Let us discuss which of those algebras are duality invariant, and in particular which symmetric bicharacters admit duality-invariant algebras. There are two natural classes of non-degenerate symmetric bicharacters, diagonal and off-diagonal:

\gamma^{(\mathrm{D})}_{t_{1},\,t_{2}}=\frac{1}{n}\,\biggl(\,\begin{matrix}t_{1}&0\\
0&t_{2}\end{matrix}\,\biggr)\qquad\qquad\text{and}\qquad\qquad\gamma^{(\mathrm{O})}_{t}=\frac{1}{n}\,\biggl(\,\begin{matrix}0&t\\
t&0\end{matrix}\,\biggr)\;.

(3.67)

Here non-degeneracy requires
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
,
t
to be invertible elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.
30
30

30

For
n
prime, they are just non-vanishing. However these two bicharacters will be used also for
n
non prime, hence
t_{1}
,
t_{2}
,
t
must be coprime with
n
.

Note that
\mathbb{B}=\{0\}
cannot lead to duality-invariant algebras because it is smaller than Lagrangian.

Consider the case of
\gamma_{t}^{(\mathrm{O})}
. First we look at Lagrangian algebras associated with subgroups
\mathbb{B}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{n}
which, according to our general analysis, need to be Lagrangian with respect to
\gamma_{t}^{(\mathrm{O})}
because
[\nu]=0
. The two subgroups
\mathbb{B}=\bigl\langle(1,0)\bigr\rangle
,
\bigl\langle(0,1)\bigr\rangle
are always Lagrangian, while
\mathbb{B}=\bigl\langle(s,1)\bigr\rangle
is Lagrangian only if

2st=0\mod n

(3.68)

which can never be satisfied if
n
is odd.
Then we look at the cases with
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{A}
. In order to satisfy
\phi\bigl(\Rad(\nu)\bigr)=N(\mathbb{B})=\{0\}
in (
3.13
) we need a discrete torsion (
3.61
) with
r\neq 0
. From (
3.52
) we find

\sigma=\biggl(\,\begin{matrix}t^{-1}r&0\\
0&-t^{-1}r\end{matrix}\,\biggr)\;.

(3.69)

The duality-invariant condition
\sigma^{2}=1
reads
(t^{-1}r)^{2}=1\text{ mod }n
, which can always be satisfied by the values
r=\pm t
.

Consider now the case of
\gamma_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{(\mathrm{D})}
. The subgroups
\mathbb{B}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{n}
are Lagrangian with respect to
\gamma_{t_{1},t_{2}}^{(\mathrm{D})}
only when
\mathbb{B}=\bigl\langle(s,1)\bigr\rangle
with
t_{1}\,s^{2}+t_{2}=0\text{ mod }n
. For
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{A}
, instead, we need a non-vanishing discrete torsion (
3.61
), and since

\sigma=\biggl(\,\begin{matrix}0&-t_{1}^{-1}r\\
t_{2}^{-1}r&0\end{matrix}\,\biggr)\;,

(3.70)

the duality-invariance condition reads
r^{2}=-t_{1}t_{2}\text{ mod }n
. This equation and the previous one for
s
do not always have solutions. For instance, if
t_{1}=t_{2}=1
, then
r
(or
s
) must be a square root of
-1
which exists for
n=2,5,13,\dots
but not for
n=3,7,11,\dots

In summary, while
\text{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{n})_{\gamma,\epsilon}
with off-diagonal bicharacter
\gamma
always trivializes the first obstruction, when the bicharacter is diagonal the category is necessarily anomalous for certain values of
n
for which the first obstruction forbids the gauging. We also notice that in all of these examples, when there is a duality-invariant Lagrangian algebra associated with
\mathbb{B}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{n}
we have
n_{\nu}=1
, while for
\mathbb{B}\cong\mathbb{A}
we have
n_{\nu}=n
.

3.

We conclude with a more complicated example which is representative of the general case
\mathbb{B}\subsetneq\mathbb{A}
but
[\nu]\neq 0
, hence
\mathbb{B}
is non-Lagrangian. Take
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{Z}_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}_{4}
which, besides the subgroups we already considered, also has the subgroup

\mathbb{B}=\bigl\{(x,2y)\bigm|x\in\mathbb{Z}_{4}\,,\;y\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\bigr\}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}

(3.71)

(as well as the similar one with the two factors swapped) hence realizing

N(\mathbb{B})=\bigl\{(0,2\tilde{y})\bigm|\tilde{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\bigr\}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\subset\mathbb{A}^{\vee}\;.

(3.72)

The most general alternating bicharacter on
\mathbb{B}
is

\chi_{\nu}=\frac{1}{4}\,\biggl(\,\begin{matrix}0&a\\
b&0\end{matrix}\,\biggr)\qquad\text{with}\qquad 2(a+b)=0\mod 4\;,

(3.73)

hence
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{4}
must be either both even or both odd. If
a,b
are both even then
\text{Rad}(\nu)=\mathbb{B}

and duality invariance cannot be satisfied. If
a,b
are odd, instead,

\text{Rad}(\nu)=\bigl\{(2z,0)\bigm|z\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}\bigr\}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{4}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\;.

(3.74)

The condition
\phi\bigl(\text{Rad}(\nu)\bigr)=N(\mathbb{B})
cannot be satisfied with the diagonal bicharacter
\gamma_{t_{1},\,t_{2}}^{(\mathrm{D})}
, while with the off-diagonal one
\gamma^{(\mathrm{O})}_{t}
the condition is met (for both the invertible elements
t=1,3
). The second condition for duality invariance involves

\sigma=\phi^{-1}\circ\psi_{\nu}=\biggl(\,\begin{matrix}tb&0\\
0&ta\end{matrix}\,\biggr)\;.

(3.75)

The condition
\sigma^{2}=1
is equivalent to
(tb)^{2}=(ta)^{2}=1
which is automatically satisfied. In this case we get
n_{\nu}=2
.

3.4
Second obstruction and equivariantization

In the previous section we rephrased the first obstruction to the gauging of a 2d duality symmetry in terms of the existence of a duality-invariant Lagrangian algebra
\mathscr{L}_{D}
in the 3d TQFT
\text{DW}(\mathbb{A})
.
Gauging
\mathscr{L}_{D}
leads to a bulk SPT phase
Y\in H^{3}\bigl(G,U(1)\bigr)
for the duality symmetry
G\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, which determines the DW twist of the corresponding
G
gauge theory as explained in (
2.8
). The total twist
\epsilon_{\text{tot}}=\epsilon\,Y
in turn determines whether a Neumann boundary condition is allowed (and
\mathcal{N}
is anomaly-free).

In order to understand the origin of
Y
we must describe in detail how to make the gauging of
\mathscr{L}_{D}
consistent with the presence of a 0-form symmetry. Naively this should amount to the requirement that
\mathscr{L}_{D}
be
G
-invariant as an object:
\Phi(\mathscr{L}_{D})=\mathscr{L}_{D}
as stressed in (
3.37
). This is however not sufficient, as the algebra
\mathscr{L}_{D}
comes with a specific choice of morphism
m:\mathscr{L}_{D}\times\mathscr{L}_{D}\to\mathscr{L}_{D}
that is associative and commutative (see Appendix
A
for the definitions) and a set of projections
\pi_{x}\in\text{Hom}(\mathscr{L}_{D},x)
. An
equivariantization
of
\mathscr{L}_{D}
is the definition of a consistent action of the 0-form symmetry on the projections that leaves
m
invariant (for more details we refer the reader to Appendix
A
and
[
84
]
for a thorough treatment). To define this structure the proper context is that of
G
-crossed MTCs
[
100
]
. In this framework a symmetry defect
U_{g}
acts on the junction spaces
V_{x,\,y}^{z}
, where
x,y,z\in\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}
label simple objects
31
31

31

Throughout this section we leave implicit that all simple objects are invertible and hence all junction spaces are one-dimensional.
, by a unitary automorphism
[\mathcal{U}_{g}]_{x,\,y}^{z}:V_{x,\,y}^{z}\to V_{g(x),\,g(y)}^{g(z)}
as

U_{g}\bigl(v_{x,\,y}^{z}\bigr)=[\mathcal{U}_{g}]_{x,\,y}^{z}\cdot v_{g(x),\,g(y)}^{g(z)}\;

(3.76)

where
v
is a chosen basis vector of
V_{x,\,y}^{z}
(see Figure
1
). The phases
[\mathcal{U}_{g}]_{x,\,y}^{z}
have to satisfy several compatibility conditions with the data of the underlying category, in particular consistency with the braiding requires

[\mathcal{U}_{g}]_{x,\,y}^{z}\;R_{x,\,y}^{z}=R_{g(x),\,g(y)}^{g(z)}\;[\mathcal{U}_{g}]_{y,\,x}^{z}\;.

(3.77)

Using the R-matrices (
3.31
) and the
G\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
action on elements of
\text{DW}(\mathbb{A})
one easily sees that this equation admits a simple solution

[\mathcal{U}_{g}]_{(a,\alpha),\,(b,\beta)}^{(a+b,\alpha+\beta)}=\alpha(b)\,,

(3.78)

for
g=\underline{1}
the generator of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

Now let us come to the equivariantization. For the algebras discussed in Section
3.3
, a consistent
32
32

32

Commutativity of the algebra requires
m_{x,\,x^{\prime}}^{z}=m_{x^{\prime},\,x}^{z}R_{x,\,x^{\prime}}^{z}
, which, in our case, becomes
m_{x,\,x^{\prime}}^{z}/m_{x^{\prime},\,x}^{z}=\chi_{\nu}(b^{\prime},b)
.
choice of
m
is

m_{x,\,x^{\prime}}^{x+x^{\prime}}=\nu(b^{\prime},\,b)\qquad\text{where}\qquad x=\bigl(b,\,\beta\psi_{\nu}(b)\bigr)\in\mathscr{L}_{D}\;.

(3.79)

In the following we will use
x,y,z,\dots
to denote elements of
\mathscr{L}_{D}
in order to lighten the notation. Working in components we expand

m=\bigoplus_{x,\,y}\,m_{x,\,y}^{z}\qquad\text{and}\qquad m_{x,\,y}^{z}\in V_{x,\,y}^{z}

(3.80)

where
z=x+y
.
The defects
U_{g}
act on the projectors
\pi_{x}:\mathscr{L}_{D}\to x
by an automorphism
\tilde{\eta}_{g}(x):\pi_{x}\to\pi_{g(x)}
as follows (see Figure
1
)

U_{g}(\pi_{x})=\tilde{\eta}_{g}(x)\cdot\pi_{g(x)}

(3.81)

g(z)
U_{g}
x
y
v_{x,y}^{z}
=\quad\;\;[\mathcal{U}_{g}]_{x,\,y}^{z}
g(z)
U_{g}
x
y
v_{g(x),\,g(y)}^{g(z)}
\mathscr{L}_{D}
U_{g}
x
\pi_{x}
=\quad\;\;\tilde{\eta}_{g}(x)
\mathscr{L}_{D}
U_{g}
x
\pi_{g(x)}
g(x)

Figure 1:
Graphical representation of the action of a symmetry defect
U_{g}
on the junction spaces
V_{x,\,y}^{z}
(above) and on the projectors
\pi_{x}
(below).

Using these transformations,
m
is invariant if
33
33

33

Here we use that all objects in the algebra
\mathscr{L}_{D}
are invertible and appear with multiplicity one in the
\text{DW}(\mathbb{A})
theory.

m_{g(x),\,g(y)}^{g(z)}=m_{x,\,y}^{z}\;[\mathcal{U}_{g}]_{x,\,y}^{z}\;\frac{\tilde{\eta}_{g}(z)}{\tilde{\eta}_{g}(x)\,\tilde{\eta}_{g}(y)}\;.

(3.82)

The equivariantization datum
\tilde{\eta}
can be neatly interpreted in cohomology. First acting with gauge transformations
\pi_{x}\to\mu(x)\,\pi_{x}
on the vector spaces associated to
\pi_{x}
and
\pi_{g(x)}
we can identify

\tilde{\eta}_{g}(x)\,\sim\,\tilde{\eta}_{g}(x)\;\frac{\mu\bigl(g(x)\bigr)}{\mu(x)}\;.

(3.83)

Second, consistency with the group composition law demands that

\tilde{\eta}_{g}(x)\;\tilde{\eta}_{h}\bigl(g(x)\bigr)=\tilde{\eta}_{gh}(x)\;.

(3.84)

Interpreting
\tilde{\eta}_{g}
as a cochain in
C^{1}\bigl(\mathscr{L}_{D},U(1)\bigr)
, so that
\tilde{\eta}\in C^{1}\bigl(G,\,C^{1}\bigl(\mathscr{L}_{D},U(1)\bigr)\bigr)
, we can rewrite (
3.84
) and (
3.83
) in terms of a differential. Using now additive notation, for the sake of clarity and for later convenience, they look, respectively, as

d_{\rho}\tilde{\eta}=0\;,\qquad\qquad\qquad\tilde{\eta}\,\sim\,\tilde{\eta}+d_{\rho}\mu\;,

(3.85)

for any
\mu\in C^{0}\bigl(G,\,C^{1}\bigl(\mathscr{L}_{D},U(1)\bigr)\bigr)\cong C^{1}\bigl(\mathscr{L}_{D},U(1)\bigr)
. Here
d_{\rho}
is a twisted differential, while
\rho
is the
G
-action on anyons.
We obtain that
\tilde{\eta}
is naturally an object in twisted group cohomology (see
e.g.

[
108
]
and Appendix
D
for a review):

\tilde{\eta}\,\in\,H^{1}_{\rho}\Bigl(G,\,C^{1}\bigl(\mathscr{L}_{D},U(1)\bigr)\Bigr)\;.

(3.86)

Restricting the solution ((
3.78
)) to elements of
\mathscr{L}_{D}
we find

[\mathcal{U}_{g}]_{x,\,x^{\prime}}^{x+x^{\prime}}=\chi_{\nu}\bigl(b,b^{\prime}\bigr)=\chi_{\nu}\bigl(\sigma(b),\sigma(b^{\prime})\bigr)^{-1}

(3.87)

where in the second step we used the relations between the symmetric and antisymmetric bicharacters (
3.55
).
Since
m_{g(x),\,g(x^{\prime})}^{g(x+x^{\prime})}=\nu\bigl(\sigma(b^{\prime}),\sigma(b)\bigr)
from (
3.79
), then (
3.82
) becomes

\frac{\nu(b,b^{\prime})}{\nu\bigl(\sigma(b^{\prime}),\sigma(b)\bigr)}=d\tilde{\eta}_{g}

(3.88)

which we recognize as the first equation in (
3.17
) with a caveat. The set of solutions for
\tilde{\eta}_{g}
, with
g=\underline{1}
, form a torsor over
\mathscr{L}_{D}^{\vee}
while the solutions of (
3.17
) are related by elements of
\bigl(\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)\bigr)^{\vee}
, therefore, strictly speaking, the solutions sets of the two equations differ. However we will see below that the two sets of equations give rise to the same second obstruction.
34
34

34

Physically the extra solutions correpond to symmetry fractionalization patterns between
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and
\mathcal{S}
for which there is no mixed ’t Hooft anomaly.

For later convenience we also notice that the set of solutions to (
3.88
) for
\tilde{\eta}
forms a torsor over

H^{1}_{\rho}\bigl(G,\,\mathscr{L}_{D}^{\vee})=H^{1}_{\rho}(G,\mathcal{S})\;,

(3.89)

whose elements we denote by
\eta
. This will be useful for the upcoming reinterpretation of the second obstruction in terms of symmetry fractionalization in Section
3.5
. All in all we found that an equivariantization of a duality-invariant Lagrangian algebra
\mathscr{L}_{D}
is specified by the choice of an element
\tilde{\eta}\in H^{1}_{\rho}\bigl(G,\,C^{1}\bigl(\mathscr{L}_{D},U(1)\bigr)\bigr)
satisfying (
3.88
), and that any two choices differ by an element
\eta
of
H^{1}_{\rho}\bigl(G,\,\mathscr{L}_{D}^{\vee})
.

Given an equivariantization
\tilde{\eta}
of
\mathscr{L}_{D}
, we ask what is the SPT phase
Y\in H^{3}\bigl(G,U(1)\bigr)
for
G
that we obtain after gauging
(\mathscr{L}_{D},\tilde{\eta})
. Indeed, the theory after gauging has a single genuine line
\mathbbm{1}
(and thus is an invertible TQFT) but also a single non-genuine topological twist line
M_{g}
for each
g\in G
. The spins
\theta_{M_{g}}
of such objects are gauge dependent by a
G
-character
[
100
]
. In the presence of a discrete torsion
Y
, the
\theta_{M_{g}}
’s do not form a
G
-character: their deviation from being a character is physical and is induced by the SPT phase
Y
. In the present case that
G\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
,
35
35

35

In order not to clutter we will suppress the label
g
in what follows, as there is only one nontrivial
G
defect anyway.

\theta_{M}
does not square to 1 but instead

\theta_{M}=\sqrt{Y(\underline{1},\underline{1},\underline{1})}\;,

(3.90)

the sign of the square root being pure gauge. We can thus detect the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
SPT phase through the gauge-invariant quantity
\theta_{M}^{2}=Y
.
We now show how to reproduce (
3.21
). A key fact is that, given a choice of equivariantization
\tilde{\eta}
for
\mathscr{L}_{D}
, there is a unique non-genuine twist line
M
after gauging
(\mathscr{L}_{D},\tilde{\eta})
. It is then possible to show, using the defining equation (
A.37
) for a twisted local module
M
that

f_{a}(b)^{-1}=\tilde{\eta}(b)\qquad\qquad\text{for}\qquad b\in\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sigma(b)=b\;,

(3.91)

where
f_{a}
is the function introduced in (
3.32
). The equation holds for all the values of
a
for which
\text{Hom}(\sigma_{a},\,M)\neq 0
.
36
36

36

To show that the result holds consider
(
A.37
)
and set
g(x_{i})=x_{i}
. The matrix
r_{L}
can then be eliminated on the two sides. Decomposing the module
M_{g}
in its components and using the formulas (
3.31
) for the
R
matrix gives the desired result.

We will use the notation
M^{(a)}
to account for the different choices one has for the equivariantization
\tilde{\eta}
: upon gauging, each choice leads to a theory with a unique non-genuine operator, however different choices lead to different SPT phases
Y
and the label
a
(whose possible values depend on
\mathscr{L}_{D}
in a complicated way) keeps track of the equivariantization chosen.

Since
\theta_{M}^{2}
must be well defined, the spins squared of the components of
M
must coincide. Since, as an object,
M^{(a)}
can be described as the orbit of the twist defect
\sigma_{a}
under fusion with the lines of
\mathscr{L}_{D}
, using the fusions in (
3.29
) we get:
37
37

37

Besides identifying twist defects related by fusion with the lines of
\mathscr{L}_{D}
, one also has to impose locality conditions, that depend on
\tilde{\eta}
(see Appendix
A
). Together these constraints single out a unique twist defect for each choice of equivariantization.

M^{(a)}=\bigoplus_{u}\sigma_{a+u}\qquad\text{where}\qquad u=b+\phi^{-1}\bigl(\beta\psi_{\nu}(b)\bigr)\qquad\text{for all}\qquad\bigl(b,\,\beta\psi_{\nu}(b)\bigr)\in\mathscr{L}_{D}\;.

(3.92)

Consistency with the existence of a unique local module requires that
\theta_{\sigma_{a}}^{2}=\theta_{\sigma_{a+u}}^{2}
,
i.e.

\theta_{M^{(a)}}^{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathbb{A}|}}\,\sum_{c\,\in\,\mathbb{A}}f_{a}(c)^{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathbb{A}|}}\,\sum_{c\,\in\,\mathbb{A}}f_{a+u}(c)^{-1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\mathbb{A}|}}\,\sum_{c\,\in\,\mathbb{A}}f_{a}(c)^{-1}\,\gamma(u,c)^{-1}\;,

(3.93)

from which we can extract some consequences. For our purposes it will be enough to consider
u=b+\phi^{-1}(\psi_{\nu}(b))
with
b\in\mathbb{B}
, we then impose

\begin{split}\theta_{M^{(a)}}^{2}&=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{B}|}\sum_{b\in\mathbb{B}}\theta_{M^{(a)}}^{2}=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{B}|\sqrt{|\mathbb{A}|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathbb{B}\\
c\in\mathbb{A}\end{subarray}}f_{a}(c)^{-1}\,\gamma(b+\phi^{-1}(\psi_{\nu}(b)),c)^{-1}\\
&=\frac{1}{|\mathbb{B}|\sqrt{|\mathbb{A}|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathbb{B}\\
c\in\mathbb{A}\end{subarray}}f_{a}(c)^{-1}\,\gamma(b,c)^{-1}\,\gamma(\phi^{-1}(\psi_{\nu}(b)),c)^{-1}\,.\end{split}

(3.94)

Any
b\in\mathbb{B}
can be split as

b=\iota(\phi^{-1}(\beta))+s(x)

(3.95)

with
\beta\in N(\mathbb{B})
and
x\in\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)
. Here
\iota
is the inclusion of
\text{Rad}(\nu)
in
\mathbb{B}
and
s:\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)\rightarrow\mathbb{B}
is a section. Using linearity of
\gamma
and that
\psi_{\nu}(\phi^{-1}(\beta))=0
we see that the only
\beta
-dependent factor in the summand is
\beta(c)
, so that the sum over
\beta
constraints
c\in\mathbb{B}
. We then have

\begin{split}\theta_{M^{(a)}}^{2}&=\frac{|\text{Rad}(\nu)|}{|\mathbb{B}|\sqrt{|\mathbb{A}|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}\\
x\in\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)\end{subarray}}f_{a}(b^{\prime})^{-1}\,\gamma(s(x),b^{\prime})^{-1}\,\gamma(\sigma(s(x)),b^{\prime})^{-1}\,.\end{split}

(3.96)

We now split also
b^{\prime}
as (
3.95
) obtaining

\theta_{M^{(a)}}^{2}=\frac{|\text{Rad}(\nu)|}{|\mathbb{B}|\sqrt{|\mathbb{A}|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta^{\prime}\in N(\mathbb{B})\\
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)\end{subarray}}f_{a}(\phi^{-1}(\beta^{\prime}))^{-1}f_{a}(s(x^{\prime}))^{-1}\,\gamma(s(x),s(x^{\prime}))^{-1}\,\gamma(\sigma(s(x)),s(x^{\prime}))^{-1}\,

(3.97)

where we noticed that
f_{a}(\phi^{-1}(\beta)+b)=f_{a}(\phi^{-1}(\beta))f_{a}(b)
for any
\beta\in N(\mathbb{B})
and
b\in\mathbb{B}
. Because of this
f_{a}
restricted on
\text{Rad}(\nu)
is a character, hence the sum over
\beta^{\prime}
yields
\theta_{M^{(a)}}^{2}=0
unless
f_{a}(\phi^{-1}(\beta^{\prime}))=1
for any
\beta^{\prime}\in N(\mathbb{B})
,
i.e.

f_{a}
must restrict to the trivial character on
\text{Rad}(\nu)
to avoid an inconsistent answer. Plugging this in we get

\theta_{M^{(a)}}^{2}=\frac{|\text{Rad}(\nu)|^{2}}{|\mathbb{B}|\sqrt{|\mathbb{A}|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta^{\prime}\in N(\mathbb{B})\\
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)\end{subarray}}f_{a}(s(x^{\prime}))^{-1}\,\gamma(s(x)+\sigma(s(x)),s(x^{\prime}))^{-1}\,

(3.98)

notice that, due to the property of
f_{a}
mentioned above, this expression is independent of the sections chosen hence we shall drop them in the following. Using (
3.55
) we rewrite

\gamma(\sigma(x),x^{\prime})=\gamma(x,\sigma(x^{\prime}))^{-1}

(3.99)

so that summing over
x
constraints
x^{\prime}
to be fixed by
\sigma
. All in all the spin of the twist defect is

\theta_{M^{(a)}}^{2}=\frac{|\text{Rad}(\nu)|}{\sqrt{|\mathbb{A}|}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)\\
\sigma(b)=b\end{subarray}}f_{a}(b)^{-1}

(3.100)

hence, due to (
3.91
), confirming that

\theta_{M^{(a)}}^{2}=\text{Arf}(\tilde{\eta})=Y\,.

(3.101)

Notice that this computation automatically provides with the proper normalization to ensure that
\Arf(\tilde{\eta})=\pm 1
.

Example.

Consider
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{n}
with off-diagonal bicharacter
\gamma_{1}^{(\mathrm{O})}
. The invariant algebra is

\mathscr{L}_{D}=\bigl\{\bigl((a_{1},a_{2});(-a_{2},a_{1})\bigr)\bigm|a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}\bigr\}\;.

(3.102)

Our choice for the functions
f_{a}
in (
3.32
) is

f_{(a_{1},\,a_{2})}(b_{1},b_{2})=\exp\biggl(-\frac{2\pi i}{n}\,b_{1}b_{2}\biggr)\,\gamma(a,b)\;.

(3.103)

From this it is simple to show that

\theta^{2}_{\sigma_{a}}=\exp\biggl(-\frac{2\pi i}{n}\,a_{1}a_{2}\biggr)\;.

(3.104)

A module
M^{(a)}
is given, as an object, by

M^{(a)}=\begin{cases}\bigoplus_{b\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}_{n}}\sigma_{b,\,a_{2}}&\text{for $n$ odd}\,,\\
\bigoplus_{b\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}_{n}}\sigma_{a_{1}+2b,\,a_{2}}&\text{for $n$ even}\,.\end{cases}

(3.105)

Imposing the spin
\theta_{\sigma_{a}}^{2}
to be constant on the orbit
M^{(a)}
strongly constrains the possible local module candidates.
One finds that for
n
odd there is only one consistent choice of module
M
, namely
M^{(0,0)}
while for
n
even there are four, corresponding to
(a_{1},a_{2})=\bigl(s_{1},\,\frac{n}{2}s_{2})
and
s_{1,2}\in\{0,1\}
. Their spins squared are:

\begin{array}[]{c||c|c|c|c}M^{(a)}&M^{(0,\,0)}&M^{(1,\,0)}&M^{(0,\,n/2)}&M^{(1,\,n/2)}\\[3.00003pt]
\hline\cr\rule{0.0pt}{13.00005pt}\theta_{M}^{2}&1&1&1&-1\end{array}

(3.106)

It is possible to check that all four satisfy the locality condition (
A.37
) for the four inequivalent choices of
\tilde{\eta}
, parametrized by
H^{1}_{\rho}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},\,\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{n})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. We will see in the next section how the same result can be obtained in terms of symmetry fractionalization.

3.5
Second obstruction and symmetry fractionalization

The discussion in the previous section gave us a description of the second obstruction from a purely bulk perspective. It however requires precise knowledge of the full categorical data of the 3d MTC, hence it is hard to generalize it to higher-dimensional cases. Moreover it leaves one conceptual problem to address: what is the physical interpretation of the different choices of equivariantization from the point of view of the boundary? We make here a proposal that solves both issues: different choices of equivariantization in the bulk lead to different ways to couple the symmetry to backgrounds fields. This goes by the name of
symmetry fractionalization
.
38
38

38

This is a slight abuse of terminology since the term “symmetry fractionalization” is commonly used to indicate the decoration of defect junctions by higher-codimension defects, while here we mix two 0-form symmetries. Yet, we use the term in order to better uniformize the 2d discussion here with the 4d one.

Even though we do not know how to turn on background fields for the non-invertible symmetry directly, we can use the vanishing of the first obstruction to reduce the problem to the discussion of inequivalent couplings to standard
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
background fields on the invertible boundary. There we also have the 0-form symmetry
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{A})/\mathscr{L}_{D}
, which crucially has a mixed anomaly with
G
. It is known
[
85
,
109
]
that in such cases the cubic
G
anomaly might not have an intrinsic value: it can be changed by choosing different symmetry fractionalization classes. Analyzing this phenomenon will lead to the required condition for the vanishing of the second obstruction.

Let us start by determining the mixed anomaly between
G
and
\mathcal{S}
. The duality action
\Phi
, which leaves
\mathscr{L}_{D}
invariant, descends to an action on the quotient
\mathcal{S}=(\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee})/\mathscr{L}_{D}
, which we already described in detail in Section
3.3
. For simplicity we consider here the case
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{A}
, so that
\mathcal{S}=\mathbb{A}^{\vee}
. The general case is qualitatively analogous and we report it in Appendix
C.2
. We use the duality isomorphism
\phi
to write the background for
\mathbb{A}^{\vee}
as
\phi(B)
with
B\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{A})
. The partition function of the invertible boundary theory coupled to a background
B
can be easily expressed in terms of the reference electric boundary:

Z_{\text{inv}}\bigl[\phi(B)\bigr]=\sum_{b\,\in\,H^{1}(X,\mathbb{A})}\exp\biggl[2\pi i\int_{X}b^{*}\nu+2\pi i\int_{X}b\cup\phi(B)\biggr]\;Z_{\mathrm{e}}[b]\;.

(3.107)

Here
b^{*}\nu\in H^{2}\bigl(X,U(1)\bigr)
is the pull-back of
\nu\in H^{2}\bigl(\mathbb{A},U(1)\bigr)
, understood in additive notation (see footnote
22
).
The duality maps
Z_{\mathrm{e}}
to the partition function of the magnetic theory
Z_{\mathrm{m}}
, which in turn can be expressed as a discrete gauging of the electric theory:

\Phi\cdot Z_{\mathrm{e}}[b]=Z_{\mathrm{m}}\bigl[\phi(b)\bigr]=\sum_{a\,\in\,H^{1}(X,\mathbb{A})}\exp\biggl[2\pi i\int_{X}\phi(a)\cup b\biggr]\;Z_{\mathrm{e}}[a]\;.

(3.108)

The action of
\Phi
on the invertible boundary can be derived combining (
3.107
) with (
3.108
), using that
\Phi
only acts on the partition functions
Z
, and it reads

\Phi\cdot Z_{\text{inv}}\bigl[\phi(B)\bigr]=\exp\biggl[2\pi i\int_{X}B^{*}\nu\biggr]\;Z_{\text{inv}}\bigl[\phi(\sigma B)\bigr]\;.

(3.109)

The overall phase stems from a mixed ’t Hooft anomaly between
G
and
\mathcal{S}
.
Crucially, from (
3.109
) we find that
G\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
acts non trivially on
\mathcal{S}
through an automorphism
\rho:G\rightarrow\text{Aut}(\mathcal{S})
such that

\rho_{\underline{1}}(B)=\sigma B\;,

(3.110)

so that the total symmetry of the invertible boundary is a semidirect product
\mathcal{S}\rtimes_{\rho}G
. Thanks to

\exp\biggl[2\pi i\int_{X}B^{*}\bigl(\nu\circ\sigma\bigr)\biggr]=\exp\biggl[-2\pi i\int_{X}B^{*}\nu\biggr]\;,

(3.111)

which is the integrated additive version of (
3.56
), the aforementioned anomaly is consistent with the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry.
Let us write the inflow action for the anomalous phase, introducing a background filed
A\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{2})
for
G
. The general construction is detailed in Appendix
D.1
. The bottom line of that discussion is that such anomalies are classified by
\mu\in H^{1}_{\rho}\bigl(\mathbb{Z}_{2},H^{2}\bigl(\mathbb{A},U(1)\bigr)\bigr)
in terms of which the 3d inflow action is

S_{\mu}=2\pi i\int_{X_{3}}\mu(A)\cup B\cup B\;.

(3.112)

In components this is defined as

\Bigl(\mu(A)\cup B\cup B\Bigr)_{ijkl}=\mu(A_{ij})\bigl(\rho_{A_{ij}}B_{jk}\,,\,\rho_{A_{ik}}B_{kl}\bigr)\;.

(3.113)

A gauge variation
A\rightarrow A+d\lambda
produces a boundary term

S_{\mu}\rightarrow S_{\mu}+2\pi i\int_{\partial X_{3}}\mu(\lambda)\cup B\cup B\;.

(3.114)

The class
\mu
can be thought of as a function
\mu:\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow H^{2}\bigl(\mathbb{A},U(1)\bigr)
satisfying the twisted cocycle condition (using addivite notation)

\rho_{g}\,\mu(h)+\mu(g)=\mu(g+h)\;,

(3.115)

and subject to the the identification

\mu(g)\,\cong\,\mu(g)+\rho_{g}\,\xi-\xi\qquad\qquad\text{for any}\qquad\xi\in H^{2}\bigl(\mathbb{A},U(1)\bigr)\;.

(3.116)

Because of the relation (
3.56
), which in additive notation reads
\sigma\cdot\nu=-\nu
, we can consistently choose

\mu(\underline{0})=0\;,\qquad\qquad\mu(\underline{1})=\nu\;.

(3.117)

Notice that this makes sense because
\Phi^{2}
leaves
Z_{\text{inv}}
invariant. With this choice, taking a background such that the pull-back of
A
to the boundary
\partial X_{3}
is
\underline{0}
and performing a gauge transformation
A\rightarrow A+d\lambda
with
\lambda\big|_{\partial X_{3}}=\underline{1}
, one reproduces the anomalous phase (
3.109
).
This construction also provides a convenient way to determine whether the anomalous phase (
3.109
) corresponds to a true anomaly or can be cancelled by a local counterterm. Indeed the latter situation occurs if and only if
\mu
is cohomologically trivial, namely

\nu=\sigma\cdot\xi-\xi

(3.118)

for some
\xi\in H^{2}\bigl(\mathbb{A},U(1)\bigr)
. In this case the anomalous phase is eliminated by modifying the action coupled to
B\in H^{1}(X_{2},\mathbb{A})
by the addition of the local counterterm

S_{\text{c.t.}}=2\pi i\int_{X_{2}}B^{*}\xi\;.

(3.119)

If there exists no
\xi
satisfying (
3.118
) then the anomalous phase cannot be cancelled and there is an anomaly. To show the power of this method, let us discuss the example of
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{n}
with diagonal symmetric bicharacter
\gamma_{1,1}^{(\mathrm{D})}
and

\nu\bigl((x_{1},x_{2}),\,(y_{1},y_{2})\bigr)=\frac{r}{n}\,x_{1}y_{2}\qquad\text{with}\qquad r^{2}=-1\mod n\;.

(3.120)

Then
\sigma
acts on
\mathbb{A}
as
\sigma(x_{1},x_{2})=(rx_{2},-rx_{1})
, and since the most general
\xi\in H^{2}\bigl(\mathbb{A},U(1)\bigr)
is represented as
\xi\bigl((x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2})\bigr)=\frac{s}{n}x_{1}y_{2}
or equivalently as
\xi\bigl((x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2})\bigr)=-\frac{s}{n}x_{2}y_{1}
then

\bigl(\sigma\cdot\xi-\xi\bigr)\bigl((x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2})\bigr)=-\frac{2s}{n}\,x_{1}y_{2}\;.

(3.121)

For
n
odd, we can always choose
s=-2^{-1}r
hence the anomalous phase can be cancelled by a local counterterm and it is not an anomaly. On the other hand, for
n
even,
r
is necessarily odd and thus no choice of
s
can cancel the anomalous phase: in this case this is a genuine anomaly.

As already argued, the question of what is the value of the pure
G\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
anomaly on the invertible boundary is not well-posed until we specify how
G
couples to a background field
A\in H^{1}(X_{2},G)
. In the boundary global variant where the full symmetry category is invertible, the presence of another 0-form symmetry
\mathcal{S}
allows one to make discrete choices for that coupling labelled by a class

\eta\in H^{1}_{\rho}(G,\mathcal{S})\;,

(3.122)

which satisfies the twisted cocycle condition
\rho_{g}\,\eta(h)+\eta(g)=\eta(g+h)
and is subject to the identification
\eta(g)\cong\eta(g)+\rho_{g}\,c-c
for any
c\in\mathcal{S}
, similarly to (
3.115
)–(
3.116
). So
\eta
specifies a (twisted) homomorphism from
G
to
\mathcal{S}
which allows one to modify the minimal coupling prescription for
A
, declaring that the latter effectively couples to the diagonal subgroup of
G
and the image
\eta(G)\subset\mathcal{S}
. The anomaly cannot be unambiguously determined until we specify
\eta
because different choices correspond to different
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
subgroups of the full 0-form symmetry group and, due to the mixed anomaly (
3.112
), they can have different anomalies.

This phenomenon is sometimes called
symmetry fractionalization
, even though the term is more often used for the mixing of a 0-form symmetry with higher-form symmetries
[
85
,
109
]
39
39

39

See,
e.g.
,
[
110
,
100
,
111
,
112
,
113
]
and references therein for discussions of symmetry fractionalization in the condensed matter literature.

(which will be relevant for the 4d/5d case), but we will use the same terminology to emphasize a unified description. The crucial point is that in general there is no canonical choice and we can only talk about differences of anomalies induced by a certain class
\eta
. This is easy to implement at the level of background fields. When
A\in H^{1}(X_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{2})
is activated, the symmetry fractionalization class changes the background
B\in H^{1}(X_{2},\mathcal{S})
to

B^{\prime}=B+A^{*}\eta=B+\eta(A)\;.

(3.123)

By plugging this expression into the mixed anomaly (
3.112
) we change the pure
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
anomaly, classified by
H^{3}\bigl(\mathbb{Z}_{2},U(1)\bigr)
, by an extra piece

S_{\text{pure}}=2\pi i\int_{X_{3}}\mu(A)\cup\eta(A)\cup\eta(A)\,\equiv\,2\pi i\int_{X_{3}}A^{*}y

(3.124)

that can be written in terms of a class
y\in H^{3}\bigl(\mathbb{Z}_{2},U(1)\bigr)
. An explicit expression for
y(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
can be derived by recasting
\mu(A)\cup\eta(A)\cup\eta(A)
as

\Bigl(\mu(A)\cup\eta(A)\cup\eta(A)\Bigr)_{ijkl}=-\mu(-A_{ij})\Bigl(\eta(A_{jk})\,,\,\rho_{A_{jk}}\eta(A_{kl})\Bigr)\;.

(3.125)

This is useful because
A
appears with only three different pairs of indices, and we conclude that

y(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})=-\mu(-g_{1})\Bigl(\eta(g_{2})\,,\,\rho_{g_{2}}\eta(g_{3})\Bigr)\;.

(3.126)

The possible non-triviality of this 3-cocycle is determined by its value at
g_{1}=g_{2}=g_{3}=\underline{1}
, and we will denote simply by
\mu
and
\eta
their values at
g=\underline{1}
. Since
\mu=\nu
and
\rho\,\eta=-\eta
we obtain

y\,\equiv\,y(\underline{1},\underline{1},\underline{1})=\nu(\eta,\eta)\;.

(3.127)

Going back to multiplicative notation, we obtain that

Y=\nu(\eta,\,\eta)\;,

(3.128)

we will see in examples that coincides with the SPT phase obtained by the equivariantization procedure in Section
3.4
.

Examples

Let us apply the general discussion to the previously discussed examples.

1.

The first example is
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{Z}_{n}
where a duality-invariant lattice is present only for
n=p^{2}
. The choice of discrete torsion
\nu
is trivial, so there is no way to shift the “bare” Frobenius-Schur indicator
\epsilon
and the second obstruction vanishes if and only if
\epsilon=1
.

2.

Next we consider TY(
\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{n}
).
Choosing the diagonal bicharacter
\gamma^{(\mathrm{D})}_{1,1}
in (
3.67
), the duality-invariant boundaries are obtained by gauging the full
\mathbb{A}
with discrete torsion
\nu
such that (see (
3.61
))

\chi_{\nu}=\frac{1}{n}\,\biggl(\,\begin{matrix}0&r\\
-r&0\end{matrix}\,\biggr)\qquad\qquad\text{with}\qquad r^{2}=-1\mod n\;.

(3.129)

Thus the action
\rho:\mathbb{Z}_{2}\rightarrow\text{Aut}(\mathbb{A}^{\vee})
is
\rho_{\underline{1}}(a_{1},a_{2})=(ra_{2},-ra_{1})
.
We look for all possible symmetry fractionalization classes
\eta\in H^{1}_{\rho}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{n})
, which are determined by
\eta\equiv\eta(\underline{1})=(x_{1},x_{2})
constrained by
x_{2}=rx_{1}
.
Taking into account the identification

(x_{1},rx_{1})\sim(x_{1},rx_{1})+(rc_{2}-c_{1},-rc_{1}-c_{2})

(3.130)

and setting
c_{2}=0
,
c_{1}=-1
we realize that
x_{1}\sim x_{1}+1
and hence all cocycles are exact:

H^{1}_{\rho}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{n})=0\;.

(3.131)

Thus the phenomenon of symmetry fractionalization is absent in this case and there is only a single equivariantization for
\mathscr{L}_{D}
. The second obstruction again vanishes if and only if
\epsilon=1
.

Choosing instead the off-diagonal bicharacter
\gamma^{(\mathrm{O})}_{1}
is more interesting. As already discussed, the duality-invariant boundaries are associated with the alternating bicharacters

\chi_{\nu}=\frac{1}{n}\,\biggl(\,\begin{matrix}0&r\\
-r&0\end{matrix}\,\biggr)\qquad\qquad\text{with}\qquad r^{2}=1\mod n\;.

(3.132)

Then
\rho_{\underline{1}}(a_{1},a_{2})=(-ra_{1},ra_{2})
and the most general cocycle
\eta\in H^{1}_{\rho}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{n})
has
\eta=(x_{1},x_{2})
with

(r-1)\,x_{1}=0\mod n\;,\qquad\qquad(r+1)\,x_{2}=0\mod n\;,

(3.133)

and is subject to the identifications
x_{1}\sim x_{1}-(r+1)c_{1}
,
x_{2}\sim x_{2}-(r-1)c_{2}
.
Without loss of generality we can take
r=1
, so that
2x_{2}=0
and
x_{1}\sim x_{1}+2
. Hence for
n
odd there is no symmetry fractionalization while for
n
even:

H^{1}_{\rho}(\mathbb{Z}_{2},\mathbb{Z}_{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{n})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}\qquad\qquad\text{($n$ even)}

(3.134)

generated by
\eta_{s_{1},\,s_{2}}=\bigl(s_{1},\frac{n}{2}s_{2}\bigr)
with
s_{1,2}\in\{0,1\}
.
A representative for
\nu
is

\nu(a,b)=\exp\biggl(\frac{2\pi i}{n}\,a_{1}b_{2}\biggr)

(3.135)

and therefore

Y=\nu(\eta,\eta)=\exp\bigl(\pi i\,s_{1}s_{2}\bigr)=\operatorname{Arf}(\tilde{\eta})\;.

(3.136)

Thus the second obstruction vanishes if and only if

\epsilon=1\quad\text{and}\quad s_{1}s_{2}=0\;,\qquad\quad\text{or}\qquad\quad\epsilon=-1\quad\text{and}\quad s_{1}s_{2}=1\;,

(3.137)

in agreement with the discussion in
[
13
]
for the case
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
and with our computations using the equivariantization of
\mathscr{L}_{D}
around (
3.106
).

4
Anomalies of duality symmetries in 3+1 dimensions

We now extend the classification of anomalies for non-invertible duality defects to the four-dimensional case. As in 2d, we find that there are two obstructions to gauging a non-invertible duality symmetry. The first obstruction again hinges upon the absence of a duality-invariant bulk Lagrangian algebra
\mathscr{L}_{D}
. This maps to the fact that the 4d theory
\mathcal{T}
coupled to the Symmetry TFT must admit a duality-invariant global variant. The second obstruction is the presence of a cubic anomaly:

\epsilon_{\text{tot}}\in\Omega_{5}^{\text{spin}}(BG)\;,

(4.1)

which can be contaminated by a mixed anomaly involving the 0-form symmetry
G
and a 1-form symmetry
\mathcal{S}
through a symmetry fractionalization mechanism similar to the 2d case, now encoded in a class
\eta\in H^{2}_{\rho}(G,\mathcal{S})
.

Well known examples of 4d theories with self-duality symmetries are the free Maxwell theory, super-Yang-Mills theories with
\mathcal{N}=4
supersymmetry and whose gauge algebra is invariant under Langlands duality (
i.e.
, ADEFG as well as
B_{2}\cong C_{2}
)
[
73
,
26
,
25
,
43
]
and various theories of class
\mathcal{S}

[
30
,
42
]
. Understanding the anomalies in these symmetries has immediate interesting consequences. For example, it has been recently observed
[
51
]
that the
\mathcal{N}=1^{*}
massive deformation of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM preserves a self-duality symmetry. The well-known results about vacuum degeneracy in
\mathcal{N}=1^{*}
can then be reinterpreted as anomaly matching conditions. A second natural application is constrain which
\mathcal{N}=3
theories can be described through a discrete gauging of
\mathcal{N}=4
, which we comment about in the conclusions.

4.1
Duality defects

Much of our analysis in Section
3
can be generalized to self-duality defects in four-dimensional theories that are self-dual under the gauging of a 1-form symmetry
\mathbb{A}

[
73
,
25
,
26
]
.
Again the self-duality must be supplied with a choice of isomorphism
\phi:\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{\vee}
. While a complete description of the underlying fusion 3-category
\mathcal{C}
is still out of reach, some of the relevant data can be spelled out explicitly.
40
40

40

The Symmetry TFT analysis offers a complementary viewpoint on the data constituting the duality category which might be easier to handle. We explain how the data we describe here are matched in Section
4.2

As stated in the introduction, this is a graded category with the grading being implemented by the duality group
G
. The fusion rules take the form

a\times\mathcal{N}_{g}=\mathcal{N}_{g}\times a=\mathcal{N}_{g}\;,\qquad\qquad\mathcal{N}_{g}(\Sigma)\times\overline{\rule{0.0pt}{8.99994pt}\mathcal{N}}_{\!g}(\Sigma)=\!\!\sum_{\gamma\,\in\,H^{1}(\Sigma,\,\mathbb{A})}\!\!a(\gamma)=C_{\mathbb{A}}(\Sigma)\;,

(4.2)

where
\mathcal{N}
is the duality interface,
\Sigma
the 3-manifold where it lives,
a
a 1-form symmetry surface, and
C_{\mathbb{A}}
the condensate of
\mathbb{A}
. The fusion of
\mathcal{N}_{g}\times\mathcal{N}_{h}
is also known, and is group-like at the level of connected components,
i.e.
forgetting the appearance of condensates (see footnote
2
).
It was analyzed in
[
43
,
42
]
.

\mathcal{N}
a
\phi(b)
=\quad\gamma(a,b)
\mathcal{N}
a
\phi(b)

Figure 2:
Braiding of lines
W^{L}_{a}
and
W^{R}_{b}
on the duality defect. Unlinking the line configuration gives rise to the symmetric bicharacter
\gamma(a,b)
.

A first piece of categorical data can be obtained noticing that the 1-form symmetry surfaces
a
can end topologically on
\mathcal{N}
thus defining topological line operators
W_{a}^{L,R}
, where
L
/
R
encode the side (Left or Right) on which the 1-form symmetry surface ends.
41
41

41

One could think of those as 2-morphisms
W^{R}_{a}:a\times\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{N}}\to\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{N}}
and
W^{L}_{a}:\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{N}}\times a\to\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{N}}
, where
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{N}}
is the identity endomorphism of
\mathcal{N}
.

These line defects must compose according to the
\mathbb{A}
group law, modulo undetectable decoupled objects:
42
42

42

The importance of modding out such decoupled TQFTs has been recently emphasized in
[
52
]
in a related context.

W^{L,R}_{a}\times W^{L,R}_{b}=W^{L,R}_{a+b}\;.

(4.3)

Following the same logic as in the Tambara-Yamagami case, we consider the braiding between endlines of 1-form symmetry surfaces
a
and
\alpha
ending on the two sides of the duality defect
\mathcal{N}
.
Using electric-magnetic duality we can map
W^{L}_{b}
to an ’t Hooft line
T^{L}_{\phi^{-1}(b)}
, which braids canonically with
W_{a}^{L}
. We conclude that the braiding between
W_{a}^{R}
and
W_{b}^{L}
is given by a symmetric bicharacter
\gamma
:

\mathcal{B}_{W_{a}^{R},\,W_{b}^{L}}=\gamma(a,b)\;,

(4.4)

where the symmetry of
\gamma
follows from the fact that we should get the same result if we worked in the magnetic frame instead. The configuration is depicted in Figure
2
.

The lines
W_{a}^{R}
and
W_{a}^{L}
form a
3d
TQFT
\mathcal{A}
, but such description is clearly non-minimal: lines of the form

\mathcal{K}_{a}=W_{a}^{R}\times W_{-a}^{L}

(4.5)

are decoupled from the bulk 1-form symmetry and constitute an undetectable sector
\mathcal{A}_{0}
. Quotienting this out gives the minimal description
\mathcal{A}_{\text{min}}
of the category of lines living on the defect.
43
43

43

Formally this is obtained by stacking the orientation reversal of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
and gauging the diagonal
\mathbb{A}
symmetry:
\mathcal{A}_{\text{min}}=(\mathcal{A}\times\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{0})/\mathbb{A}
.

This produces, in general, a set of lines
L_{a}
forming a minimal
\mathbb{A}
TQFT
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{A},\,q}

[
114
]
, with
q
the quadratic refinement of the symmetric bilinear form determined by
\gamma
. This resonates with previous results obtained from the Symmetry TFT perspective
[
43
]
.

Finally, as in the two-dimensional case, we can associate to
\mathcal{N}
a pure
G
anomaly
\epsilon
. This is as an higher analogue of the Frobenius-Schur indicator. For
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
such analogy is exact, as
\epsilon
is reflected in a time reversal anomaly on the duality defect
[
115
,
116
]
.
Alternatively,
\epsilon
can be understood as a standard
G
’t Hooft anomaly on four-manifolds with trivial
H_{2}(X,\mathbb{A})
. On these manifolds,
\mathcal{N}_{g}
behaves as a standard invertible symmetry according to the fusion rules (
4.2
).
At the level of the Symmetry TFT, the presence of a nontrivial
\epsilon
gives a DW twist for the theory
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C})
.
All in all, we find that the known data defining a self-duality category in 4d, or at least a subset of them, are given by a pure anomaly
\epsilon
for the self-duality group and a symmetric bicharacter
\gamma:\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}\rightarrow U(1)
.

In the ensuing analysis we will make two simplifying assumptions. First, we will consider duality defects on spin manifolds,
w_{2}(TX)=0
. The classification of discrete gauging operations (global variants of a gauge theory) is different on non-spin manifolds, as the set of discrete theta angles is larger.
44
44

44

As an illuminating example, consider
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{Z}_{n}
with
n
even. On generic manifolds, discrete torsion terms are classified by
H^{4}\bigl(B^{2}\mathbb{Z}_{n},U(1)\bigr)=\mathbb{Z}_{2n}
, while on spin manifolds the order-two element of this group vanishes due to the Wu formula
B\cup B=B\cup w_{2}(TX)\text{ mod }2
. This discussion generalizes to arbitrary
\mathbb{A}
in a straightforward manner.

Physically this amounts to the possibility of assigning a well-defined spin to lines as this cannot be screened by heavy neutral fermions
[
117
]
. This restriction has physical consequences on the obstruction theory outlined above: some duality defects can be anomaly free on spin manifolds, but anomalous in the presence of a nontrivial
w_{2}
.
45
45

45

Loosely speaking, this is some kind of mixed anomaly with gravity, due to the dependence on
w_{2}(TX)
.
.
As a prototypical example, consider the
\mathfrak{su}(2)

\mathcal{N}=4
SYM theory. This admits an
S
-invariant global variant
SO(3)_{-}
on spin manifolds. On non-spin (but orientable) manifolds this variant splits into
SO(3)_{-}^{\text{b}}
and
SO(3)_{-}^{\text{f}}
, where b/f (bosonic/fermionic) refer to the spin of the generator of the lattice of genuine lines. According to
[
117
]
(Appendix C) the two objects are interchanged by
S
. Thus, although the duality symmetry in
SU(2)

\mathcal{N}=4
SYM might be non-anomalous on spin manifolds, it is anomalous on generic orientable manifolds.
46
46

46

We will briefly comment on the interpretation of this fact from the point of view of gapped phases in Appendix
F
.

Our second assumption is to consider duality defects for which
G
does not contain fermion parity. This for example excludes the vanilla
S
-duality of the
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM theory, for which
S^{4}=(-1)^{F}
, but includes the situation where
S
is twisted by a discrete R-symmetry
[
51
]
. At the practical level, this implies that the relevant cobordism classification for cubic
G
anomalies is given by
\Omega_{5}^{\text{spin}}(BG)
as opposed to
\Omega_{5}^{\text{spin}-G}(\text{pt})
. Both groups have been computed
e.g.
in
[
118
,
119
]
.

4.2
Symmetry TFT and Lagrangian algebras

The Symmetry TFT for 4d duality defects can be described in close analogy with the 2d case
[
81
,
43
]
. We start from a 5d Dijkgraaf-Witten theory for a 1-form symmetry
\mathbb{A}
with trivial twist. This has topological surface operators labelled by pairs
(a,\alpha)\in\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}
with antisymmetric canonical braiding

\mathcal{B}_{(a_{1},\alpha_{1}),(a_{2},\alpha_{2})}=\alpha_{1}(a_{2})\;\alpha_{2}(a_{1})^{-1}\quad\in\,U(1)\;.

(4.6)

As in three dimensions, the 5d pure 2-form gauge theory for
\mathbb{A}
enjoys
electric-magnetic
duality, corresponding to a choice of isomorphism
\phi
. There is an important difference, though, with respect to the 3d case. The most general ansatz for a duality is

\begin{array}[]{cccc}S:&\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}&\rightarrow&\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}\\
&(a,\alpha)&\mapsto&\bigl(I\circ\phi^{-1}(\alpha),\,\phi(a)\bigr)\end{array}

(4.7)

for some automorphism
I:\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}
to be determined. Let
\gamma:\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}\rightarrow U(1)
be the bicharacter associated with
\phi
, namely
\gamma(a,b)=\phi(a)b
, then
S
preserves the braiding if and only if

\gamma\bigl(a,\,I\circ\phi^{-1}(\alpha)\bigr)\;\gamma\bigl(\phi^{-1}(\alpha),\,a\bigr)=1\;.

(4.8)

This equation may have multiple solutions, depending on the Abelian group
\mathbb{A}
, but here we limit ourselves to EM dualities that can be defined universally. If
I
is the identity then
\gamma
is an antisymmetric non-degenerate bicharacter, which however does not exist for all Abelian groups
47
47

47

For instance
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{Z}_{n}
,
n\neq 2
does not admit any.

and thus we will not study this case any further. On the other hand, if
I
is the inversion

I(a)=-a

(4.9)

then
\gamma
must be a symmetric non-degenerate bicharacter, which always exists. We will thus consider this case in the following.
48
48

48

Other automorphisms
I
may exist and lead to EM dualities for certain Abelian groups
\mathbb{A}
.

With this choice of definition,
S
is an order-four automorphism:

S^{2}(a,\alpha)=(-a,-\alpha)\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad S^{2}=C\;,

(4.10)

where we defined the charge-conjugation operator
C:\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}
. The 5d
\text{DW}(\mathbb{A})
theory enjoys a larger set of 0-form symmetries, for any group
\mathbb{A}
. Indeed we can define another generator

T:(a,\,\alpha)\mapsto\bigl(a+\phi^{-1}(\alpha),\,\alpha\bigr)\;,

(4.11)

and in this way construct an order-three automorphism of
\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}
:

CST:(a,\,\alpha)\mapsto\bigl(\phi^{-1}(\alpha),\,-\alpha-\phi(a)\bigr)\qquad\text{such that}\qquad(CST)^{3}=\mathbbm{1}\;.

(4.12)

The Symmetry TFT for the duality or triality defects is then defined by gauging the group
G
generated by
S
or
CST
, respectively. This gauging admits a choice of discrete torsion, which on spin manifolds is classified by

\epsilon\,\in\,\Omega_{5}^{\text{spin}}(BG)\;,

(4.13)

and can be thought of as the higher analogue of the Frobenius-Schur indicator we introduced before.

The same argument for the first obstruction corresponding to the absence of
G
-invariant Lagrangian algebras in the
\text{DW}(\mathbb{A})
theory carries over to the 5d case. We are thus led to study the properties of gapped boundaries of the pure 2-form gauge theory for
\mathbb{A}
. These are labelled by two discrete choices, as in 2d:

•

a subgroup
\mathbb{B}\subset\mathbb{A}
to be gauged;

•

a class
[\nu]\in H^{4}\bigl(B^{2}\mathbb{B},U(1)\bigr)
specifying the discrete torsion.

Recall that in 2d the discrete-torsion classes are classified by alternating bicharacters. The analog here is the identification of
H^{4}\bigl(B^{2}\mathbb{B},U(1)\bigr)
with the dual of the universal quadratic group
\Gamma(\mathbb{B})
(see
[
120
,
108
]
for details):

H^{4}\bigl(B^{2}\mathbb{B},U(1)\bigr)\,\cong\,\Gamma(\mathbb{B})^{\vee}\;.

(4.14)

This means that any discrete torsion class
[\nu]
can be represented by a quadratic function
q_{\nu}:\mathbb{B}\rightarrow U(1)
.
The group
\Gamma(\mathbb{B})
is equipped with a quadratic function
\mathcal{Q}:\mathbb{B}\rightarrow\Gamma(\mathbb{B})
such that for any Abelian group
V
, any quadratic function
q:\mathbb{B}\rightarrow V
factorizes as
q=\tilde{q}\circ\mathcal{Q}
with
\tilde{q}:\Gamma(\mathbb{B})\rightarrow V
a group homomorphism. The topological term implementing the discrete torsion is

S_{\text{torsion}}=\int_{X_{4}}B^{*}\nu=\int_{X_{4}}\tilde{q}\bigl(\mathfrak{P}(B)\bigr)\;,

(4.15)

where
\mathfrak{P}(B)=B^{*}\mathfrak{P}\in H^{4}\bigl(X_{4},\Gamma(\mathbb{B})\bigr)
is the pull-back of the universal Pontryagin square class
\mathfrak{P}\in H^{4}\bigl(B^{2}\mathbb{B},\Gamma(\mathbb{B})\bigr)
whilst
\tilde{q}
is the homomorphism associated with the quadratic function
q_{\nu}
.

Crucially, if
X_{4}
is a four-dimensional spin manifold, then two discrete torsions
\nu
,
\nu^{\prime}
leading to two quadratic functions
q_{\nu}
,
q_{\nu^{\prime}}
which are different quadratic refinements of the same bicharacter lead to the same topological term
[
114
,
117
]
:
\int_{X_{4}}B^{*}\nu=\int_{X_{4}}B^{*}\nu^{\prime}
.
Thus by working on spin manifolds we can safely label topological manipulations of the boundary theory in terms of a choice of subgroup
\mathbb{B}\subset\mathbb{A}
and of symmetric bicharacter
\chi_{\nu}:\mathbb{B}\times\mathbb{B}\rightarrow U(1)
. Then most of the results will be closely analogous to the 2d/3d case, just replacing antisymmetric with symmetric bicharacters.

As explained, on spin manifolds we can label the Lagrangian algebras
\mathscr{L}_{\mathbb{B},[\nu]}
in terms of the data
(\mathbb{B},\chi_{\nu})
. The corresponding gapped boundary has a 1-form symmetry

\mathcal{S}=\bigl(\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}\bigr)/\mathscr{L}_{\mathbb{B},[\nu]}\;.

(4.16)

One can easily adapt the 3d discussion in order to explicitly write the form of the general Lagrangian algebra. The symmetric bicharacter
\chi_{\nu}:\mathbb{B}\times\mathbb{B}\rightarrow U(1)
induces a group homomorphism
\psi_{\nu}:\mathbb{B}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}^{\vee}
as in the 3d case.
Given a pair
(\mathbb{B},\chi_{\nu})
we construct the Lagrangian algebra
\mathscr{L}_{\mathbb{B},[\nu]}\subset\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}
as

\mathscr{L}_{\mathbb{B},[\nu]}=\Bigl\{\bigl(b,\,\beta\psi_{\nu}(b)\bigr)\in\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}\Bigm|b\in\mathbb{B}\,,\quad\beta\in N(\mathbb{B})\Bigr\}\;.

(4.17)

This has cardinality
|\mathbb{A}|
and is Lagrangian since
\mathcal{B}_{(b_{1},\beta_{1}),(b_{2},\beta_{2})}=\chi_{\nu}(b_{2},b_{1})\>\chi_{\nu}(b_{1},b_{2})^{-1}=1
, where
(b,\beta)
is a shorthand for
\bigl(b,\beta\psi_{\nu}(b)\bigr)
and we used the symmetry of
\chi_{\nu}
.
As in the 3d case (see Appendix
B
) one can show that all Lagrangian algebras of the 5d
\text{DW}(\mathbb{A})
theory are of this form.

4.3
First obstruction

After fixing a choice of electric-magnetic duality, we ask what are the conditions for a duality-invariant Lagrangian algebras
\mathscr{L}_{D}=\Phi(\mathscr{L}_{D})
to exist. We will study two cases:
\Phi=S
(duality) and
\Phi=CST
(triality). Other cyclic 0-form symmetry groups, when they exist, can be treated similarly. As we previously showed, all Lagrangian algebras are of the form (
4.17
).
To verify whether a lattice is
\Phi
-invariant, as in 3d we impose that the pairing between
\mathscr{L}
and
\Phi(\mathscr{L})
be trivial. The analysis is analogous to the 3d case. For both choices of
\Phi
, we find the necessary condition

\phi\bigl(\text{Rad}(\nu)\bigr)=N(\mathbb{B})\;,

(4.18)

where
\text{Rad}(\nu)
is the kernel of
\psi_{\nu}
. As in the 3d case this implies that
|\mathbb{B}|^{2}=k|\mathbb{A}|
for some positive integer
k\in\mathbb{N}
and still
\mathbb{B}
cannot be smaller than Lagrangian. Notice however since
\chi_{\nu}
is now symmetric rather than antisymmetric we cannot conclude that
|\mathbb{A}|
(and in particular
k
) is a perfect square. Indeed we will see explicit counterexamples, hence showing that in higher categories the obstruction from non-integer quantum dimensions of
[
12
]
is not true.

The remaining conditions depend on
\Phi
and are listed below.

Duality.

The automorphism
\sigma=\phi^{-1}\psi_{\nu}
of
\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)
must satisfy

\sigma^{2}=-1\;.

(4.19)

In particular
\sigma
allows to relate the two symmetric bicharacters as

\gamma\bigl(\sigma(a),\,b\bigr)=\chi_{\nu}(a,b)\ .

(4.20)

From the two equations above it follows that
\sigma
is an order-two automorphism of the group of symmetric bilinear forms on
\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)
:

\chi_{\nu}\bigl(\sigma(a),\sigma(b)\bigr)\;\chi_{\nu}(a,b)=1\;.

(4.21)

Triality.

The automorphism
\tau=\phi^{-1}\psi_{\nu}
must satisfy

1+\tau+\tau^{2}=0\;.

(4.22)

It is simple to show that the above implies that
\tau
is an order-three operation:
\tau^{3}=1
.
Also in this case, the restriction to
\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)
of

\gamma\bigl(\tau(a),\,b\bigr)=\chi_{\nu}(a,b)

(4.23)

holds.
Using the two above equations it follows that
\tau
is an order-three automorphism of the group of symmetric bilinear forms on
\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)
:

\chi_{\nu}\bigl(\tau^{2}(a),\tau^{2}(b)\bigr)\;\chi_{\nu}\bigl(\tau(a),\tau(b)\bigr)\;\chi_{\nu}(a,b)=1\;.

(4.24)

Examples

1.

Let us study the case of
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{Z}_{n}
with the standard symmetric bicharacter
\gamma(a_{1},a_{2})=\exp\bigl(\frac{2\pi i}{n}\,a_{1}a_{2}\bigr)
. Consider a factorization
n=pq
and a subgroups

\mathbb{B}=\left\{bq\ |\ b=0,...,p-1\right\}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}

(4.25)

so that
N(\mathbb{B})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. Since duality invariance requires
\mathbb{B}
to contain
\phi^{-1}(N(\mathbb{B}))
as a subgroup,
q
must divide
p
and we set
p=\ell q
.
A choice of
\psi_{\nu}
is associated with another symmetric bicharacter defined on
\mathbb{B}
:

\chi_{\nu}(b_{1},b_{2})=\exp\left(\frac{2\pi ir}{p}\,b_{1}b_{2}\right)\ ,

(4.26)

where
r\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}
.
Notice that
\text{Rad}(\nu)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{\gcd(r,p)}
hence imposing
\phi\bigl(\text{Rad}(\nu)\bigr)=N(\mathbb{B})
forces
\text{gcd}(r,p)=q
, namely
r=sq
with
\gcd(\ell,s)=1
. Furthermore, since
\gamma(qb_{1},qb_{2})=\exp{\left(\frac{2\pi iq}{p}b_{1}b_{2}\right)}
over
\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{q}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{l}
we have

\sigma(b)=\phi^{-1}\psi_{\nu}(b)=sb\ \text{mod}(\ell)

(4.27)

Thus we find that:

1.

On spin manifolds, there is a duality-invariant
\mathscr{L}_{D}
for
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{Z}_{n}
if
n=\ell q^{2}
and
-1
is a quadratic residue
\text{mod}(\ell)
:

s^{2}=-1\ \text{mod}(\ell)\,.

(4.28)

2.

On spin manifolds, there is a triality-invariant
\mathscr{L}_{D}
for
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{Z}_{n}
if
n=\ell q^{2}
and the equation

s^{2}+s+1=0\ \text{mod}(\ell)\,,

(4.29)

admits solutions.

These results coincide with the recent classification
[
49
]
of 4d topological
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
gauge theories that are duality or triality invariant on spin manifolds. As in the 3d case, we provide a precise connection between the two approaches in Appendix
F
.

We notice a simple number theoretic consequence of this result. The duality invariance condition can never be satisfied with
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{Z}_{n}
if
n
is a multiple of 4. Indeed it is simple to see that if
n=2m
then
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
such that
s^{2}=-1\ \text{mod}(2m)
must be
1\ \text{mod}(2)
. But then if
m
is even
s=2x+1
cannot satisfy
s^{2}=-1\ \text{mod}(2m)
. Analogously triality invariance cannot be satisfied if
n
is multiple of
9
.

2.

Another interesting case to consider is
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
which is the one-form symmetry group of
\text{Spin}(4k)
gauge theory. On
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
there are four symmetric non-degenerate quadratic forms

\gamma^{(D)}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\,,\ \ \gamma^{(O)}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\ \ \gamma^{+}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\ \ \gamma^{-}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}.

(4.30)

In this case
-1
acts as the identity on
\mathbb{A}
and duality is an involution. Thus given any choice of
\gamma
the first obstruction is cancelled by choosing
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{A}
,
\sigma=1
and
\chi_{\nu}=\gamma
.
The case of triality is slightly more involved. Let us consider
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{A}
. It is simple to show that the only two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
isomorphisms
\tau
satisfying
\tau^{2}+\tau+1=0
are
\tau^{\pm}=\big(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\big)
,
\big(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&1\end{smallmatrix}\big)
which are inverses to each other. If
\gamma=\gamma^{(D)}
we can solve the triality obstruction by taking
\chi_{\nu}=\gamma^{\pm}
and
\tau=\tau^{\pm}
, similarly if
\gamma=\gamma^{\pm}
we can take
\chi_{\nu}=\gamma^{(D)}
and
\tau=\tau^{\mp}
. On the other hand, if
\gamma=\gamma^{(O)}
,
\gamma(\tau^{\pm}(a),\,b)
is not symmetric and the obstruction is present for
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{A}
. Let us then consider
\gamma=\gamma^{(O)}
and
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. Since
N(\mathbb{B})
is also
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
we must have that
\mathbb{B}=\phi(N(\mathbb{B}))
. It is simple to verify that taking
\mathbb{B}
to be the diagonal
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
this is indeed satisfied. We conclude that the first obstruction for
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}
vanishes for both duality and triality.

This example, combined with the previous one, allows to discuss the first obstruction for
\mathcal{N}=4

\text{Spin}(2m)
SYM (and its global variants). Recall that the one-form symmetry group is:

\mathbb{A}=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}_{4},\,\ \ \ &\text{if}\ m=2k+1\,,\\
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2},\,\ \ \ &\text{if}\ m=2k\,.\end{cases}

(4.31)

We thus find that the first obstruction identically vanishes in all of the relevant cases.

4.4
Second obstruction and symmetry fractionalization

While in absence of duality- (or triality-) invariant Lagrangian algebras the non-invertible self-duality symmetry is anomalous, when such invariant algebra does exist the anomalies are determined by those on the invariant boundary, where the symmetry is invertible. The philosophy is the same as in the 2d/3d case: due to a mixed anomaly between the 1-form symmetry
\mathcal{S}=\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}/\mathscr{L}_{D}
and the invertible duality symmetry
G
we can shift the value of the pure
G
anomaly by changing the symmetry fractionalization class
\eta\in H^{2}_{\rho}(G,\,\mathcal{S})
. We now determine the mixed anomaly in the simpler case
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{A}
, the generalization to proper subgroups being straightforward but technically tedious.

Duality.

In the case of
\Phi=S
and so
G=\mathbb{Z}_{4}
the invariant partition function is given by:
49
49

49

For simplicity we omit the normalization factors due to gauging.

Z_{\text{inv}}\bigl[\phi(B)\bigr]=\sum_{b\,\in\,H^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{A})}\exp\biggl(2\pi i\int_{X_{4}}b^{*}\nu+2\pi i\int_{X_{4}}\phi(B)\cup b\biggr)\;Z_{\mathrm{e}}[b]\;,

(4.32)

where
Z_{\mathrm{e}}
is the partition function corresponding to the reference electric boundary condition, while
\nu
is defined through a bicharacter
\chi_{\nu}
such that

\gamma\bigl(\sigma(a),\,b\bigr)=\chi_{\nu}(a,b)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sigma^{2}=-1\;.

(4.33)

The action of
S
-duality on
Z_{\text{inv}}
is easily determined using the action of
S
-duality on the electric theory:

S\cdot Z_{\mathrm{e}}[B]=\sum_{a\,\in\,H^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{A})}\exp\biggl(2\pi i\int_{X_{4}}\phi(B)\cup a\biggr)\;Z_{\mathrm{e}}[a]\;.

(4.34)

We find

S\cdot Z_{\text{inv}}\bigl[\phi(B)\bigr]=G_{\nu}\exp\biggl(2\pi i\int_{X_{4}}B^{*}\nu\biggr)\;Z_{\text{inv}}\bigl[\phi(\sigma B)\bigr]\;,

(4.35)

where
G_{\nu}\equiv\sum_{b\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{B})}\exp\bigl(2\pi i\int_{X}b^{*}\nu\bigr)
.
Here, assuming that
X_{4}
is spin, we used the simplifying relation

\exp\biggl(2\pi i\int_{X_{4}}B^{*}\bigl(\nu\circ\sigma\bigr)\biggr)=\exp\biggl(-2\pi i\int_{X_{4}}B^{*}\nu\biggr)\;.

(4.36)

Assuming that
X_{4}
is simply connected (and thus
H^{2}(X_{4},\mathbb{Z})
has no torsion classes) and spin, one can show that the Gauss sum
G_{\nu}
is equal to 1
[
49
]
.
In a similar way we can verify that

S^{2}\cdot Z_{\text{inv}}\bigl[\phi(B)\bigr]=Z_{\text{inv}}\bigl[-\phi(B)\bigr]=C\cdot Z_{\text{inv}}\bigl[\phi(B)\bigr]\;.

(4.37)

Eqn. (
4.35
) implies that the
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
symmetry generated by
S
acts on the 1-form symmetry of the theory through
\sigma
,
i.e.
, the symmetry is a split 2-group with nontrivial action
\rho:G\to\text{Aut}(\mathbb{A})

[
121
,
120
,
108
]
given by
\rho_{\underline{1}}(a)=\sigma\,a
.
Furthermore, the overall phase
\exp\bigl(2\pi i\int_{X}B^{*}\nu\bigr)
should be thought of as encoding a mixed anomaly

\mu\,\in\,H^{1}_{\rho}\Bigl(\mathbb{Z}_{4},\,H^{4}\bigl(B^{2}\mathbb{A},\,U(1)\bigr)\Bigr)\qquad\qquad\text{where}\qquad\qquad\mu(\underline{1})=\nu

(4.38)

and
\underline{1}
is the generator of
G=\mathbb{Z}_{4}
, much as in the 2d case.

Triality.

For
\Phi=CST
and so
G=\mathbb{Z}_{3}
we have the same expression (
4.32
) for
Z_{\text{inv}}\bigl[\phi(B)\bigr]
, but with the class
\nu
now satisfying (
4.24
) in terms of a
\tau
such that
\tau^{2}+\tau+1=0
. T-duality acts on the electric boundary as

T\cdot Z_{e}[B]\equiv\exp\biggl(-2\pi i\int_{X_{4}}B^{*}\gamma\biggr)Z_{e}[B]\,.

(4.39)

Then

(CST)\cdot Z_{\mathrm{e}}[B]=\exp\biggl(2\pi i\int_{X_{4}}B^{*}\gamma\biggr)\sum_{a\,\in\,H^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{A})}\exp\biggl(2\pi i\int_{X_{4}}\phi(B)\cup a\biggr)\;Z_{\mathrm{e}}[a]

(4.40)

with
B^{*}\gamma
any class stemming from a quadratic refinement of
\gamma
,
i.e.
the Pontryagin square induced by
\gamma
, and we find

(CST)\cdot Z_{\text{inv}}\bigl[\phi(B)\bigr]=G_{\gamma+\nu}\,\exp\biggl(2\pi i\int_{X_{4}}B^{*}\nu\biggr)\;Z_{\text{inv}}\bigl[\phi(\tau B)\bigr]

(4.41)

Here we used that, on spin manifolds,
\exp\bigl[2\pi i\int B^{*}\bigl(\nu+\nu\circ\tau+\nu\circ\tau^{2}\bigr)\bigr]=1
. It also holds that

(CST)^{3}\cdot Z_{\text{inv}}\bigl[\phi(B)\bigr]=Z_{\text{inv}}\bigl[\phi(B)\bigr]\;.

(4.42)

As before, the result is interpreted by saying that the split 2-group is twisted by the
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
symmetry and the overall phase comes from a mixed anomaly

\mu\,\in\,H^{1}_{\rho}\Bigl(\mathbb{Z}_{3},\,H^{4}\bigl(B^{2}\mathbb{A},\,U(1)\bigr)\Bigr)\qquad\qquad\text{where}\qquad\qquad\mu(\underline{1})=\nu\;.

(4.43)

We thus conclude that, similarly to the
3
d case, the
5
d mixed anomaly is determined by a class

\mu\in H^{1}_{\rho}\Bigl(G,\,H^{4}\bigl(B^{2}\mathbb{A},U(1)\bigr)\Bigr)\cong H^{1}_{\rho}(G,\Gamma(\mathbb{A})^{\vee})

(4.44)

namely a function from
G
to the group of quadratic functions over
\mathbb{A}
satisfying

\rho_{g}\,\mu(h)+\mu(g)=\mu(g+h)\;

(4.45)

and subject to the the identification

\mu(g)\,\cong\,\mu(g)+\rho_{g}\,\xi-\xi\qquad\qquad\text{for any}\qquad\xi\in H^{4}\bigl(B^{2}\mathbb{A},U(1)\bigr)\;.

(4.46)

The full detailed derivation of the anomaly inflow is given in Appendix
D.2
and we find

S_{\mu}=2\pi i\int_{X_{5}}\mu(A)\cup\mathfrak{P}_{\rho}(B)\;.

(4.47)

To reproduce the anomalous phase arising in the boundary theory we have to compare this phase with the boundary term arising in
S_{\mu}
from
A+d\lambda
when we set the pull-back of
A
to the boundary to zero, as well as the boundary value of
\lambda
equal to the element of the group
G
for which we compute the variation. This determines all the values of
\mu(g)
for
g\in G
. We can check that the consistency (
4.45
) of these values is satisfied.
In the case of duality
G=\mathbb{Z}_{4}
, since
\nu
satisfies
\nu\bigl(\sigma(a),\sigma(b)\bigr)=-\nu(a,b)
, we deduce that

\mu(\underline{1})=\mu(\underline{3})=\nu\;,\qquad\qquad\mu(\underline{0})=\mu(\underline{2})=0\;.

(4.48)

It is obvious that (
4.45
) is satisfied.

For triality
G=\mathbb{Z}_{3}
the crucial relation is

\gamma(a,b)\,+\,\gamma\bigl(\tau(a),\tau(b)\bigr)\,+\,\gamma\bigl(\tau^{2}(a),\tau^{2}(b)\bigr)=0\;.

(4.49)

By looking at the anomalous phases that we got this implies that

\mu(\underline{0})=0\;,\qquad\quad\mu(\underline{1})(a,b)=\gamma\bigl(\tau(a),\tau(b)\bigr)\;,\qquad\quad\mu(\underline{2})(a,b)=\gamma\bigl(\tau(a),\tau(b)\bigr)+\gamma(a,b)\;.

(4.50)

Among the consistency relations (
4.45
), the only non-trivial (and independent) ones to check are:

\tau\mu(\underline{1})+\mu(\underline{1})=\mu(\underline{2})
,
\tau\mu(\underline{2})+\mu(\underline{1})=0
and
\tau^{2}\mu(\underline{2})+\mu(\underline{2})=\mu(\underline{1})
, which are indeed satisfied thanks to (
4.49
).

Given such a mixed anomaly, we are now able to discuss the pure
G
anomaly. The philosophy is the same as in the 2d/3d case: combining the choice of symmetry fractionalization with the mixed anomaly we can induce an extra contribution to the pure anomaly for the invertible duality symmetry. The details are however slightly different.

In 4d symmetry fractionalization is classified by
\eta\in H^{2}_{\rho}(G,\mathbb{A})
, which, as opposed to the 2d case where it corresponds to the choice of a
G
subgroup of the full symmetry, here it corresponds to the choice of a 1-form symmetry defect
\eta(g,h)\in\mathbb{A}
inserted along the junction of the intersection of
g,h
and
gh
defects. This amounts to redefine the coupling of the 0-form symmetry to a background, prescribing that
B
is shifted to

B^{\prime}=B+A^{*}\eta\in H^{2}_{\rho}(X,\mathbb{A})\;.

(4.51)

By plugging this expression into the mixed anomaly (
4.47
) we shift the pure
G
anomaly by an extra piece

S_{\text{pure}}=2\pi i\int_{X_{5}}\mu(A)\cup\mathfrak{P}_{\rho}(A^{*}\eta)\,\equiv\,2\pi i\int_{X_{5}}A^{*}y

(4.52)

that can be written in terms of a class
y\in H^{5}\bigl(G,U(1)\bigr)
. In order to work out an explicit expression for this class we rely on a working assumption. We note that the Pontryagin square operation, when the homology group
H_{2}(X_{5},\mathbb{Z})
is torsion-free, can be written as a cup product
50
50

50

The expression (
4.53
) should be interpreted as follows. One writes
\mathbb{A}=\oplus_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}
and lift
A^{*}\eta
to
\oplus_{i}\mathbb{Z}
, which is always possible for finite Abelian groups. In
\oplus_{i}\mathbb{Z}
we can take the product among the various components of the lift, then (
4.53
) is obtained restricting the result to
\Gamma\left(\oplus_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}\right)=\bigoplus_{i}\Gamma\left(\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}\right)\oplus\bigoplus_{i<j}\mathbb{Z}_{n_{i}}\otimes\mathbb{Z}_{n_{j}}
. If
X_{5}
has torsion 1-cycles the Pontryagin square is not a cup product and in order to write it in components we need Steenrod’s cup products (see
e.g.

[
108
]
).

[
120
]
:

\mathfrak{P}_{\rho}(A^{*}\eta)=A^{*}\eta\cup A^{*}\eta\;.

(4.53)

On the other hand the pure
G
anomaly is non-trivial when the homology group
H_{1}(X_{5},\mathbb{Z})
contains torsion
[
119
]
. Therefore, in order to do the computation, we pick a bulk spin manifold
X^{*}_{5}
with torsion 1-cycles but with torsion-free 2-cycles so as to write (
4.52
) as

S_{\text{pure}}=2\pi i\int_{X^{*}_{5}}\mu(A)\cup A^{*}\eta\cup A^{*}\eta\;.

(4.54)

Then it is easy to conclude that

y\bigl(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4},g_{5}\bigr)=\langle-\mu(-g_{1})\,,\eta(g_{2},g_{3})\,\rho_{g_{2}+g_{3}}\,\eta(g_{4},g_{5})\rangle\;.

(4.55)

where the product in the second entry should be interpreted as in footnote
50
. When the second entry is the image of a quadratic function
\gamma:\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\Gamma(\mathbb{A})
the above expression can be rewritten in a simpler form using the universal property defining
\Gamma(\mathbb{A})
(see the discussion around (
4.14
)). In particular if we can find a representative for
\eta
that is invariant under the
\rho
action, setting
g_{2}=g_{4}
and
g_{3}=g_{5}
, we have

y\bigl(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{2},g_{3}\bigr)=\langle-\mu(-g_{1})\,,\eta(g_{2},g_{3})\,\eta(g_{2},g_{3})\rangle=-\mu(-g_{1})(\eta(g_{2},g_{3}))\;.

(4.56)

Examples

We now discuss how this general story applies to examples where
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{Z}_{n}
and
G
is either
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
or
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
, namely duality and triality respectively. This has some consequence for the anomaly structure of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM theories with gauge group
\mathrm{SU}(n)
at
\tau=i,e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}}
respectively.

Several technical details on the computations of the twisted cohomology groups are based on the following known result (see
e.g.
[
122
]
). If
G\cong\mathbb{Z}_{k}
, denoting
f=\rho_{\underline{1}}\in\text{Aut}(\mathbb{A})
(note that
f^{k}=1
), then

H^{n}_{\rho}(G,\mathbb{A})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\displaystyle\frac{\text{Ker}(1-f)}{\text{Im}(1+f+f^{2}+...+f^{k-1})}&\ \ \ \ \ \text{if $n$ is even}\\
\\
\displaystyle\frac{\text{Ker}(1+f+f^{2}+...+f^{k-1})}{\text{Im}(1-f)}&\ \ \ \ \ \text{if $n$ is odd}\end{array}\right.

(4.57)

The symmetry fractionalization classes are classified by
H^{2}_{\rho}(G,\mathbb{A})
, and we notice that in both the duality and triality examples we have

1+f+f^{2}+...+f^{k-1}=0

(4.58)

by virtue of the relations
\sigma^{2}=-1
,
\tau^{2}+\tau+\tau=1
. Hence for us

H^{2}_{\rho}(G,\mathbb{A})=\text{Ker}(1-f)=\left\{a\in\mathbb{A}\ |\ \rho_{\underline{1}}(a)=a\right\}=\text{Fix}_{\rho_{\underline{1}}}(\mathbb{A})\ .

(4.59)

This also gives a hint for the form of the explicit representatives of the non-trivial twisted cocycles as

\eta_{x}(\underline{1},\underline{1})=x\ ,\ \ \ x\in\text{Fix}_{\rho_{\underline{1}}}(\mathbb{A})

(4.60)

Duality.

For the case of duality
G\cong\mathbb{Z}_{4}
we have

\rho_{\underline{1}}(a)=ta\ ,\ \ \ t^{2}=-1\ \text{mod}(n)\ .

(4.61)

Using (
4.59
) we get

H^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},\mathbb{Z}_{n})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbb{Z}_{2}&\ \ \ \ \text{if $n$ is even}\\
0&\ \ \ \ \text{if $n$ is odd}\end{array}\right.

(4.62)

and in the even case the cocycles can be represented

\eta_{s}(\underline{1},\underline{1})=\eta_{s}(\underline{3},\underline{3})=\eta_{s}(\underline{1},\underline{3})=\eta_{s}(\underline{3},\underline{1})=\frac{n}{2}s\ ,\ \ \ \ \ s=0,1

(4.63)

with all the other values vanishing. By setting
n=2m
, the pure anomaly is determined by the value of the 5-cocycle
Y\in H^{5}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},U(1))
in
g_{1}=...=g_{5}=\underline{1}
and we get

Y=q_{\nu}(\eta_{s}(\underline{1},\underline{1}))=e^{2\pi its^{2}\frac{m}{4}}\,.

(4.64)

We conclude that for
n
odd the pure duality anomaly on the invertible boundary is the bare one, given by
\epsilon\in H^{5}(\mathbb{Z}_{4},U(1))\cong\mathbb{Z}_{4}
, while for
n
even the cancellation depends on the possible values of
Y
. Recall that the first obstruction never vanishes when
m
is even. Therefore the possible values of
Y
are

Y=\exp\left(\dfrac{\pi i}{2}t(2k+1)\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \text{for }n=2(2k+1)\,.

(4.65)

In the
\mathcal{N}=4
theory with gauge group
\mathrm{SU}(n)
at
\tau=i
the non-invertible duality symmetry is anomalous whenever it is intrinsically non-invertible, on spin manifolds we have given the relevant condition for
\mathbb{A}=\mathbb{Z}_{n}
around equation (
4.28
).
If the defect is non-intrinsically non-invertible the anomaly automatically vanishes provided we combine the duality with an appropriate R-symmetry rotation in order to have a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
operation (see
e.g.

[
123
,
89
]
). Indeed following
[
124
]
and using that
\Omega^{spin}_{5}(B\mathbb{Z}_{4})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{4}
one gets
[
51
]

\epsilon=60(n-1)-24(n^{2}-1)\ \text{mod}(4)=0\,,

(4.66)

therefore one should choose the trivial fractionalization class to cancel the second obstruction. One could also consider other definitions of S-duality which do not involve the R-symmetry, in such cases the relevant bordism group
\Omega^{\text{spin}-\mathbb{Z}_{8}}(pt)=\mathbb{Z}_{32}\oplus\mathbb{Z}_{2}
is larger and our techniques would need to be refined in order to appropriately account for the cubic anomaly.

A similar conclusion applies to Maxwell theory, for which
S^{4}=1
and the anomaly
56\ \text{mod}(4)=0
also identically vanishes.

Triality.

In the triality case
G\cong\mathbb{Z}_{3}
,

\rho_{\underline{1}}(a)=ta\ ,\ \ \ \ t^{2}+t+1=0

(4.67)

for which we get

H^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{Z}_{3},\mathbb{Z}_{n})\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbb{Z}_{3}&\ \ \ \ \text{if $n=0\ $mod(3) }\\
0&\ \ \ \ \text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(4.68)

and the (non)trivial cocycles are

\eta_{s}(\underline{1},\underline{1})=\eta_{s}(\underline{2},\underline{2})=\eta_{s}(\underline{1},\underline{2})=\eta_{s}(\underline{2},\underline{1})=\frac{n}{3}s\ ,\ \ \ \ \ s=0,1,2

(4.69)

with all the other values vanishing. Setting
n=3m
, the class
Y\in H^{5}(\mathbb{Z}_{3},U(1))\cong\mathbb{Z}_{3}
is determined by
51
51

51

One can easily check that, when
n=3m
is also even, so
m=0\text{ mod }(2)
, the choice of quadratic refinement for
q_{\gamma}
is immaterial.

Y=\left[\mu(\underline{2})\Bigl(\eta_{s}(\underline{1},\underline{1}),\,\rho_{\underline{2}}\,\eta_{s}(\underline{1},\underline{1})\Bigr)\right]^{-1}=q_{\gamma}\left(\eta_{s}(\underline{1},\underline{1})\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\exp{\left(2\pi i\dfrac{k}{3}\right)}&\text{if $m=2k$}\\
&\\
\exp{\left(2\pi i\dfrac{4k+2}{3}\right)}&\text{if $m=2k+1$}\end{array}\right.\,.

(4.70)

Again we can apply these results to the case of triality symmetry appearing in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM at
\tau=e^{2i\pi/3}
. The triality defect is non-intrinsic when there exist
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{n}
such that
1+t+t^{2}=0\ \text{mod}(n)
. When this is the case we can ask about the second obstruction. To apply our methods we are not forced to combine the naive
CST
operation with an R-symmetry rotation to eliminate fermion parity, since
(CST)^{3}=\mathbbm{1}
. Then, by the same token as the duality case and knowning that
\Omega^{spin}_{5}(B\mathbb{Z}_{3})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{9}
, we have

\epsilon=60(n-1)\ \text{mod}(9)=-3(n-1)\ \text{mod}(9)\,.

(4.71)

Notice that
Y
is valued in the
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
subgroup of the
\mathbb{Z}_{9}
anomaly group, then to compare
Y
to the
\epsilon
above we need to multiply by
3
. When
n=1\ \text{mod}(3)
then
\epsilon=0
and there is no choice of fractionalization, therefore the second obstruction vanishes. For
n=2\ \text{mod}(3)
we find
\epsilon=6
and the triality defect is always anomalous. Finally when
n=0
mod
3
we have
\epsilon=3
and a simple computation shows that the second obstruction can be cancelled only when
n=3m
with
m=1\text{ mod }(3)
.

In Maxwell theory instead the anomaly is
56\ \text{mod}(9)=2
and cannot be cancelled by any choice of symmetry fractionalization. We conclude that the triality symmetry in Maxwell theory is always anomalous due to the second obstruction.

5
A check from dimensional reduction

As a check of our results, we show that the obstruction theory of Section
4
is consistent with the one for Tambara-Yamagami categories upon dimensional reduction on an orientable 2-manifold
W
.
We treat explicitly the case that
W
is a torus
T^{2}
, but the generalization to any Riemann surface
\Sigma_{\mathfrak{g}}
is straightforward. Physically this should be expected, indeed the simplest example of a 4d theory enjoying self-duality is Maxwell theory, which upon compactification on
T^{2}
reduces to the theory of two compact bosons.
52
52

52

Plus a decoupled 2d Maxwell sector that we ignore. Such a sector has a 1-form and a
(-1)
-form symmetry (associated to a
2\pi
shift of the theta angle), associated to the 0-form and 2-form symmetries of the Symmetry TFT.

In this example the complexified gauge coupling
\tau
is mapped to the position of the 2d CFT on the Narain moduli space. Such a theory is well known to enjoy Tambara-Yamagami-type symmetries if the point on the conformal manifold is chosen appropriately
[
15
]
.

Compactifying the 5d Dijkgraaf-Witten theory for
\mathbb{A}
on the torus is a simple exercise. The resulting 3d TQFT has a 1-form symmetry
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{\vee}
where

\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}=\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}\;,

(5.1)

together with a 0-form and a 2-form symmetry, both being
\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}
, which we neglect in the following discussion.
Given a choice
\phi
for the isomorphism that enters into the 5d duality symmetry, the defect
\Phi
also implements a
\mathbb{Z}_{4}
symmetry in 3d:

\Phi(a_{1},\,a_{2};\,\alpha_{1},\,\alpha_{2})=\bigl(-\phi^{-1}(\alpha_{2}),\,\phi^{-1}(\alpha_{1});\,-\phi(a_{2}),\,\phi(a_{1})\bigr)\;,

(5.2)

where
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}
and
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{\vee}
.
To get a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry we compose this transformation with the internal S-duality of the torus, which also squares to charge conjugation and sends
(a_{1},\,a_{2};\,\alpha_{1},\,\alpha_{2})\to(a_{2},\,-a_{1};\,\alpha_{2},\,-\alpha_{1})
. The resulting
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry, which we dub
\widetilde{\Phi}
acts as:

\widetilde{\Phi}(a_{1},\,a_{2};\,\alpha_{1},\,\alpha_{2})=(\phi^{-1}(\alpha_{1}),\,\phi^{-1}(\alpha_{2});\,\phi(a_{1}),\,\phi(a_{2}))\,,

(5.3)

or, using the
\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\widetilde{\Phi}:\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{\vee}&\longrightarrow&\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}\times\widetilde{\mathbb{A}}^{\vee}\\
(\widetilde{a},\widetilde{\alpha})&\longrightarrow&(\widetilde{\phi}^{-1}(\widetilde{\alpha}),\widetilde{\phi}(\widetilde{a}))\end{array}

(5.4)

with
\widetilde{\phi}:\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{\vee}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}
given by
\widetilde{\phi}(a_{1},a_{2})=(\phi(a_{1}),\phi(a_{2}))
.

First and second obstruction upon dimensional reduction

We now discuss how the first obstruction in 5d is mapped to the first obstruction in 3d language after compactification. Clearly not all Lagrangian algebras
\mathscr{L}
in the 3d description can descend from a 5d description, so we must first characterize them.
Recall that, in 5d, algebras where described by a choice of subgroup
\mathbb{B}
of
\mathbb{A}
together with a discrete torsion
[\nu]\in H^{4}\bigl(B^{2}\mathbb{B},\,U(1)\bigr)
. Upon reduction on
T^{2}
this should map to a specific class
[\tilde{\nu}]\in H^{2}\bigl(B\widetilde{\mathbb{B}},\,U(1)\bigr)
, where
\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}=\mathbb{B}\times\mathbb{B}
.
Expanding the 5d background
B=B_{1}\theta_{1}+B_{2}\theta_{2}
with
\theta_{i}
a basis of
H^{1}(T^{2},\mathbb{Z})
(we neglect the 0-form and 2-form symmetries), we find:

\int_{T^{2}}B^{*}\nu=B_{1}\cup_{\nu}B_{2}-B_{2}\cup_{\nu}B_{1}\;,

(5.5)

where
\cup_{\nu}
is the cup product induced by the symmetric bilinear form
\chi_{\nu}
. The bicharacter corresponding to
\tilde{\nu}
is then, in matrix and additive notation,

\chi_{\tilde{\nu}}=\biggl(\begin{matrix}0&\chi_{\nu}\\
-\chi_{\nu}&0\end{matrix}\biggr)\;.

(5.6)

A 3d Lagrangian algebra
\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}
induced from 5d then is of the form

\widetilde{\mathscr{L}}=\Bigl\{\bigl(\tilde{b},\,\tilde{\beta}\psi_{\tilde{\nu}}(\tilde{b})\bigr)\Bigm|\tilde{b}\in\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}\,,\quad\tilde{\beta}\in N(\widetilde{\mathbb{B}})\Bigr\}\;,

(5.7)

where
\psi_{\tilde{\nu}}:\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}\to\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}^{\vee}
is the homomorphism associated with the antisymmetric bicharacter (
5.6
).
Since
\text{Rad}(\tilde{\nu})=\text{Rad}(\nu)\times\text{Rad}(\nu)
the 5d condition
\phi(N(\mathbb{B}))=\text{Rad}(\nu)
implies
\phi(N(\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}))=\text{Rad}(\widetilde{\nu})
in 3d.
On the other hand, the map
\tilde{\sigma}=\tilde{\phi}{}^{-1}\psi_{\tilde{\nu}}
is given by:
