\tilde{\sigma}=\biggl(\begin{matrix}0&\sigma\\
-\sigma&0\end{matrix}\biggr)\;,

(5.8)

which is an involution
\tilde{\sigma}^{2}=1
. We have thus shown that solutions to the first obstruction in 5d always descend to solutions to the first obstruction in 3d.

Let us now discuss the second obstruction. We notice that the 5d discrete torsion
\epsilon
, when reduced on
T^{2}
, trivializes. This is because the torus (as well as any Riemann surface) does not have torsion 1-cycles. Thus it is not possible to detect the 5d second obstruction in 3d after compactification on a Riemann surface. Indeed, from the point of view of symmetry fractionalization, we have
G\cong\mathbb{Z}_{n}
and for any Abelian group
\mathbb{A}
we get

H^{1}_{\rho}(\mathbb{Z}_{n},\,\mathbb{A})=\text{Ker}(1+f)/\text{Im}(1-f)\;,

(5.9)

with
f=\rho_{\underline{1}}
.
Applying this to the case
\mathbb{A}=\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}/N(\widetilde{\mathbb{B}})
and
f=\tilde{\sigma}
it is simple to prove that the twisted cohomology group is trivial for any choice of
\mathbb{B}
.
53
53

53

Using that
\sigma^{2}=-1
we find that
\text{Ker}(1+\tilde{\sigma})
is spanned by elements
(b_{1},b_{2})\in\widetilde{\mathbb{B}}/N(\widetilde{\mathbb{B}})
such that
b_{2}=\sigma(b_{1})
. An element of
\text{Im}(1-\tilde{\sigma})
instead is of the form
(b_{1},b_{2})=\bigl(x-\sigma(y),\,\sigma(x)+y\bigr)
. A simple manipulation shows that this is equivalent to
b_{2}=\sigma(b_{1})
.

Thus there are no fractionalization classes and therefore the second obstruction always trivializes.

6
Conclusions and applications

Let us conclude by mentioning some immediate applications of our results, as well as some interesting open problems.

4d
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{N}=3}
theories.

It has been appreciated in the past that a class of 4d
\mathcal{N}=3
theories may be obtained from a discrete gauging of the
\mathcal{N}=4
duality symmetry for special values of
\tau

[
123
,
89
]
. More precisely, given a
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
subgroup of
SL(2,\,\mathbb{Z})
and a fixed coupling
\tau_{k}
, where
k=2,3,4,6
,
54
54

54

To be precise, since the duality group is
\text{Mp}(2,\,\mathbb{Z})
the discrete groups are actually
\mathbb{Z}_{3},\,\mathbb{Z}_{4},\,\mathbb{Z}_{8},\,\mathbb{Z}_{12}
as charge conjugation squares to fermion number
C^{2}=(-)^{F}
. The combined duality - R symmetry transformation however lies is
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
with
k
as in the main text.

we can combine this transformation with a
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
R-symmetry rotation in the Cartan of
SU(4)
so that the combined action preserves
\mathcal{N}=3
supersymmetry. As the gauge coupling
\tau=\tau_{k}
must be fixed to its self-dual value, these theories have no exactly marginal deformation and are inherently strongly coupled.
The case of
k=2
is special, as the symmetry is charge conjugation, hence it preserves the full
\mathcal{N}=4
supersymmetry, and is invertible.
We will thus concentrate on the cases
k=4
(corresponding to the
S
transformation) and
k=3
(corresponding to the
CST
transformation) and gauge group
SU(n)
.
As the duality symmetry is non-invertible, it must be gauged together with (a subgroup of) the
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
1-form symmetry and our results imply that this is only consistent if the first obstruction vanishes. Thus there is a severe constraint on the possible
\mathcal{N}=3
theories which can be obtained in this way. For example our results show that there is no such theory for
n=3
and
k=4
.
We must also check the vanshing of the second obstruction.
The joint duality/R-symmetry anomaly is given by
[
51
]
:

60(n-1)-24(n^{2}-1)\ \begin{cases}&\text{mod }4\,,\ \ \ \ \text{if}\ k=4\\
&\text{mod }9\,,\ \ \ \ \text{if}\ k=3\end{cases}\,.

(6.1)

For the duality case the cubic anomaly is identically trivial, thus the vanishing of the first obstruction is a sufficient condition for the gauging to be consistent. For triality instead it is given by
6\text{ mod }9
when
n=3m+2
and is zero otherwise. It has been checked in
[
51
]
that this anomaly identically trivializes when the first obstruction vanishes. Therefore also in the triality case the gauging is consistent if the first obstruction vanishes. This also implies that, when
n=3m
, we must choose the trivial fractionalization class
\eta\in H^{2}_{\rho}(\mathbb{Z}_{3},\,\mathbb{Z}_{3m})
.

In some special cases the S-fold construction of
[
125
]
gives rise to discrete gaugings of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM
[
126
]
. These are engineered by 2 D3-branes probing a
k=3,\,4,\,6
S-fold and lead to a discrete gauging of
SU(3),\,SO(5)
and
G_{2}

\mathcal{N}=4
SYM respectively. Our analysis can be applied to the first two cases which, following the discussed examples, indeed are free of anomalies for triality and duality respectively. It would certainly be interesting to understand whether our methods can give some insight also on
\mathcal{N}=3
theories which cannot be obtained by a discrete gauging procedure from
\mathcal{N}=4
and, in particular, if they enlarge the list of generalized symmetries of S-folds described recently in
[
127
,
128
]
.

A mixed anomaly.

We have mentioned in Section
3.3
that the space of duality-invariant Lagrangian algebras is larger on spin manifolds. Similarly one can argue, for example following
[
49
]
, that the first obstruction in the 4d case has less solutions if the spacetime
X
is not spin.
This should be rephrased as the presence of a mixed ’t Hooft anomaly between the non-invertible symmetry
\mathcal{N}
and gravity, sourced by a nontrivial second Stiefel-Whitney class
w_{2}(X)
. A well known example is the symmetry
\text{TY}(\mathbb{Z}_{2})_{1,1}
of the Ising CFT. As a bosonic symmetry this is anomalous as the first obstruction cannot be cancelled. However, if we consider it on spin manifolds
X
only, the obstruction is absent since the bulk algebra
\mathscr{L}_{D}=\left\{(0,0),\,(1,1)\right\}
is manifestly duality invariant. Such an algebra can only be condensed on spin manifolds as
\theta_{(1,1)}=-1
. On the field theory side it is well known
[
12
,
15
,
129
]
that fermionizing the Ising CFT into a Majorana fermion the duality symmetry
\mathcal{N}
becomes the invertible
(-1)^{F_{L}}
which is anomaly free. A similar example in 4d, as already stated before, if the
\mathcal{N}=4

SU(2)
SYM theory, whose duality symmetry is anomaly-free on spin manifolds (after combining it with an R-symmetry rotation) it is anomalous by the first obstruction when
X
is non-spin.
It would be nice to make this idea more precise.

Duality-invariant RG flows.

In both 2d and 4d, duality-symmetric theories allow for a plethora of interesting RG flows which preserve the non-invertible symmetry. In the former case they have been studied in
[
15
]
, while in the latter an initial study has appeared recently
[
51
]
. As in the 2d case, the anomalies for the duality symmetry can lead to strong constraints on the possible low energy phases. A simple example is the
\mathcal{N}=1^{*}

[
130
,
131
,
132
,
133
,
134
]
deformation of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM at
\tau=i
, which, in the presence of the first obstruction, necessarily leads in the IR either to spontaneous symmetry breaking of the non-invertible symmetry, or to a self-dual Coulomb phase
[
51
]
. A related problem deserving further study in the light of our results is the deformation of the
SU(2),\,SU(3),\,SU(4)

\mathcal{N}=4
theory by the Konishi operator. This must lead in the IR either to an
\mathcal{N}=0
CFT or to chiral symmetry breaking in order to match the cubic
\mathrm{SU}(4)
anomaly. Consistency of these scenarios with the intricate pattern of non-invertible symmetries and their anomalies might allow to put stringent constraints on the possible IR phases. This problem is currently under investigation.

Intrinsic versus anomalous.

In our work we have seen that, in the context of duality symmetries, the concept of “instrinsic”
[
81
]
implies that the duality symmetry is anomalous.
Such concept is not unique to duality symmetries, and can be rephrased as the statement that the symmetry category
\mathcal{C}
is not Morita equivalent to any category of the form
n\text{Vec}_{G}
for some (higher) group
G
.
It would be interesting to understand how far the relationship between ’t Hooft anomalies and intrinsic defects extends.

Duality-invariant boundary conditions.

It is known
[
71
,
72
]
that the presence of an ’t Hooft anomaly for a symmetry
\mathcal{C}
forbids the existence of a
\mathcal{C}
-invariant boundary condition.
55
55

55

Strictly speaking the argument of
[
72
]
only applies to 2d. However, given a representation of the higher dimensional ’t Hooft anomalies in terms of defect configurations, it should be possible to extend it to general symmetry categories.
Our results can in principle be used to constrain the existence of duality-invariant conformal boundary conditions, building on the results of
[
135
,
136
,
137
]
56
56

56

See also
[
59
]
for more details on the action of the non-invertible duality symmetry on boundary conditions in Maxwell theory.
for
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM and free Maxwell theory, respectively. This problem is currently under investigation
[
138
]
.

Appendix A
Gauging in fusion categories

In this appendix we briefly review well known material about gauging in Fusion categories and Modular tensor categories (possibly extended by zero-form symmetry). A complete review of the underlying formalism can be found in
[
5
,
100
]
respectively.

Gauging and algebras.

Gauging a generalized symmetry in two dimensions corresponds to the definition of a special symmetric Frobenius algebra
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathcal{C}
. This is described by a triplet:

\mathcal{A}\equiv(\mathcal{A},\,m\,,\eta)\,,\ \ \ m\in\Hom(\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A},\,\mathcal{A})\,,\ \ \ \eta\in\Hom(\mathbbm{1},\,\mathcal{A})\,,

(A.1)

where
\mathcal{A}=\bigoplus_{x_{i}}Z_{i}(\mathcal{A})\,x_{i}
is an object in
\mathcal{C}
, and we define
Z_{i}(\mathcal{A})=\dim(\Hom(\mathcal{A},\,x_{i}))
. We use
x_{i}
to denote the simple objects in
\mathcal{C}
.
The maps
\pi_{i}
are projectors
\pi_{i}:\mathcal{A}\to x_{i}
onto the simple components of
\mathcal{A}
and can be used to recast the commuting diagrams below in tensor-valued expressions.
The algebra morphism
m
satisfies

F_{\mathcal{A},\,\mathcal{A},\,\mathcal{A}}^{m}=1

(A.2)

(
m
trivializes the associator). Furthermore
\eta\circ m=\text{id}_{\mathcal{A}}
. We will henceforth suppress
\eta
for simplicity. The algebra also has a dual structure

(\Delta,\,\bar{\eta})\,,\ \ \ \Delta\in\Hom(\mathcal{A},\,\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A})\,,\ \ \ \bar{\eta}\in\Hom(\mathcal{A},\,\mathbbm{1})\,.

(A.3)

Satisfying
\Delta\circ m=\text{id}_{\mathcal{A}}
. Furthermore
\Delta
and
m
satisfy the so called Frobenius condition

(A.5)

such an algebra is called
commutative
.
The gauging of a symmetry
\mathcal{A}
is implemented by a thick enough network of
\mathcal{A}
defects, with morphisms
m
and
\Delta
at three-valent junctions.
To understand the symmetry of the theory after gauging we must introduce the concept of modules. First let us introduce the category of (left)
\mathcal{A}
-modules
\text{Mod}_{\mathcal{A}}
.
Its elements are doublets
(M,r_{L})
with
M
an object an
r_{L}\in\Hom(\mathcal{A}\times M,\,M)
a morphism allowing the algebra to end on
M
.
r_{L}
must satisfy a natural compatibility condition:

(A.6)

This equation allows to interpret
r_{L}
as a sort of representation of the algebra
\mathcal{A}
on
r_{L}
. Physically the category
\text{Mod}_{\mathcal{A}}
describes an
\mathcal{A}
-invariant boundary condition. In two dimensions the category describing the symmetry after gauging
\mathcal{A}
is the bimodule category
\text{Bimod}_{\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{A}}
of
\mathcal{A}
bimodules. A bimodule
(B,\,r_{L},\,r_{R})
is both a left and a right module for
\mathcal{A}
such that the left and right actions commute:

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(A.7)

Finally, the symmetry after gauging
\mathcal{A}
in 3d is described by local modules
\text{Mod}^{loc}_{\mathcal{A}}
of the commutative algebra
\mathcal{A}
. These are modules which are compatible with the braiding with
\mathcal{A}
. In particular given a left module morphism
r_{L}
we define the right morphism
r_{R}
by

r_{R}=r_{L}\circ b

(A.8)

With the consistency condition:

\displaystyle\hbox to141.81pt{\vbox to48.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 70.90268pt\lower-23.56944pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{15.1232pt}{-1.17496pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(A.9)

This implements the intuition that objects after the gauging must braid trivially with
\mathcal{A}
. It is known that the dimension of the category of local modules is:

\dim(\text{M}^{loc}_{\mathcal{A}})=\frac{\dim(\mathcal{C})}{\dim(\mathcal{A})}\,,\ \ \ \dim(\mathcal{A})=\sum_{i\,\in\,\text{simple}}Z_{i}(\mathcal{A})\ \dim(x_{i})\,.

(A.10)

Since the dimension of a fusion category must be
\geq 1
there is a notion of maximality in gauging commutative algebras, which implies that:

\dim(\mathcal{A})\leq\dim(\mathcal{C})\,,

(A.11)

when the inequality is saturated the algebra
\mathcal{A}
is called
Lagrangian
and is denoted by the letter
\mathscr{L}
. There are more or less standard techniques to construct the category of modules, which employ the fact that the formal tensor product:

\text{Ind}_{\mathcal{A}}(x_{i})=\mathcal{A}\times x_{i}\,,

(A.12)

gives a (reducible) left
\mathcal{A}
module. These modules are called “induced” and the construction of
\text{Mod}_{\mathcal{A}}
boils down to the decomposition of induced modules. The interested reader can consult
[
5
,
105
]
for a review of these techniques.

Theories with 0-form symmetry.

We will also need some material about 3d theories enriched with a zero-form symmetry
G
. These are the so-called G-crossed extensions and we refer to
[
100
]
for a complete review.
A G-crossed extension is described by a graded tensor category

\mathcal{C}=\bigoplus_{g\,\in\,G}\mathcal{C}_{g}

(A.13)

with
\mathcal{C}_{g}
the
g
-twisted sector of the zero-form symmetry. This can be thought of as a two-category
\Sigma\mathcal{C}
with a single connected component
|\pi_{0}(\Sigma\mathcal{C})|=1
, the twist defects giving a base of homomorphisms
\sigma_{g}\in\Hom(U_{g},\,\mathbbm{1})
. We will use
x_{i}
to denote objects in the untwisted sector, while
\sigma_{g,\,i}
denote (simple) twist defects.
The fusion product on the twist defects is graded:

\mathcal{C}_{g}\times\mathcal{C}_{h}\subset\mathcal{C}_{gh}\,.

(A.14)

The zero-form symmetry naturally acts on the defects in
\mathcal{C}
via an automorphism of the fusion algebra:

U_{g}\left[X_{h}\right]=g(X)_{h^{-1}gh}\,\in\mathcal{C}_{h^{-1}gh}\,,

(A.15)

we will henceforth restrict to an abelian zero-form symmetry
G
. The symmetry also acts on the junction spaces
V_{(g,i),\,(h,j)}{}^{(gh,k)}
by (unitary) isomorphisms

\mathcal{U}_{g}:V_{(g_{1},i),\,(g_{2},\,j)}{}^{(g_{1}g_{2},k)}\to V_{(g_{1},g(i)),\,(g_{2},g(j))}{}^{(g_{1}g_{2},g(k))}\,,

(A.16)

while the
G
composition law is encoded in a morphism

\lambda_{x_{i}}(g,h):\,g(h(x_{i}))\to gh(x_{i})\,.

(A.17)

The category comes with a graded associator
\alpha
and a graded braiding isomorphism:

b:X_{g}\times Y_{h}\to g(Y)_{h}\times X_{g}\,,

(A.18)

also satisfying
G
-crossed versions of the pentagon and hexagon equations.
The number of simple objects
\sigma_{g,\,i}
in the
g
-twisted sector is equal to the number of
g
-invariant local lines
x_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
,
g(z_{i})=x_{i}
. This follows from modularity of the Hilbert space on
T^{2}
with
G
backgrounds.
The dimension of each graded category
\mathcal{C}_{g}
is
G
invariant, thus:

\dim(\mathcal{C}_{g})=\dim(\mathcal{C}_{0})\,,\ \ \ \dim(\mathcal{C})=|G|\dim(\mathcal{C}_{0})\,.

(A.19)

Gauging and equivariantization.

There are two natural operations which can be introduced in this setting. The first is gauging (A subgroup of) the zero-form symmetry
G
. This leads to a larger modular tensor category
\mathcal{C}/G
which has dimension:

\dim(\mathcal{C}/G)=|G|\dim(\mathcal{C})\;,

(A.20)

\mathcal{C}/G
has an anomaly free one-form symmetry
\text{Rep}(G)=G^{\vee}
which gives charge to the liberated
g
-twisted sectors. The category after gauging hence is still graded by this charge:

\mathcal{C}/G=\bigoplus_{g\in G}\mathcal{D}_{g}\,.

(A.21)

The way in which simple objects of
\mathcal{C}/G
are constructed is familiar from the theory of orbifolds. A simple object
\sigma_{g,\,i}
is equivariantized into an orbit:

\Sigma_{g,\,i}=\bigoplus_{h\in G/\text{Stab}(\sigma_{g,i})}h\left(\sigma_{g,i}\right)\,,

(A.22)

where

\text{Stab}(X)=\left\{g\in G:g(X)=X\right\}\,,

(A.23)

is the stabilizer subgroup. This object can furthermore be dressed by dual symmetry lines which carry a representation
\pi
of
\text{Stab}(\sigma_{g,i})
. We thus get:

|\text{Stab}(\sigma_{g,i})|\ \ \text{objects}\ \ \ \Sigma_{g,i}^{\pi}\,.

(A.24)

The second is to gauge an algebra
\mathcal{A}_{0}\subset\mathcal{C}_{0}
. Let
H\subset G
be the stabilizer of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
:

H=\left\{g\in G:g(\mathcal{A}_{0})=\mathcal{A}_{0}\right\}

(A.25)

we say that
\mathcal{A}_{0}
preserves a subgroup
H
of the zero form symmetry.
To specify an
H
-invariant algebra we must also associate a consistent
H
-action to the data
(m,\,\eta)
. This constitutes an
equivariantization
of
\mathcal{A}_{0}
and is generally not unique nor it is guaranteed to exist. The required conditions are simple to summarize. First we require the stabilizer
H
to leave the algebra morphism fixed

m_{g(x),\,g(y)}^{g(z)}=m_{x,\,y}^{z}\;[\mathcal{U}_{g}]_{x,\,y}^{z}\;\frac{\tilde{\eta}_{g}(z)}{\tilde{\eta}_{g}(x)\,\tilde{\eta}_{g}(y)}\;.

(A.26)

requires the definition of maps
\tilde{\eta}_{g}(x)
on the projectors
\pi_{x}:\mathcal{A}_{0}\to x
:

\pi_{x_{i}}\to\tilde{\eta}_{g}(x_{i})\,\pi_{g(x_{i})}\,,

(A.27)

furthermore the maps
\tilde{\eta}_{g}(x_{i})
must compose nicely under the
H
action, namely

\tilde{\eta}_{g}(x_{i})\,\tilde{\eta}_{h}(g(x_{i}))=\lambda_{x_{i}}(g,h)\,\tilde{\eta}_{gh}(x_{i})\,.

(A.28)

A solution to equation (
A.26
), (
A.28
) is not guaranteed to exist, and its existence is tied to the splitting of a certain short exact sequence
[
84
]
.
Even if a solution exists it must be modded out by the appropriate gauge transformations. Suppose that
\Hom(\mathcal{A}_{0},\,x_{i})
is at most one dimensional, then
\eta_{g}
is a one-cochain and we can redefine:

\pi_{x_{i}}\to\mu(x_{i})\,\pi_{x_{i}}\,,\ \ \ \tilde{\eta}_{g}(x_{i})\to\tilde{\eta}_{g}(x_{i})\frac{\mu(x_{i})}{\mu(g(x_{i}))}\,.

(A.29)

With this settled gauging
\mathcal{A}_{0}
preserves an
H
subgroup of the zero-form symmetry. The resulting category is:

\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{A}_{0}=\bigoplus_{h\in H}\mathcal{C}_{h}/\mathcal{A}_{0}\,,

(A.30)

each entry having dimension:

\dim(\mathcal{C}_{h}/\mathcal{A}_{0})=\dim(\mathcal{C}_{0})/\dim(\mathcal{A}_{0})\,,

(A.31)

by consistency. We now describe in some detail the objects of the twisted category
\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{A}_{0}
. Clearly since
\mathcal{A}_{0}
has trivial grading it is possible to define twisted module categories:

\text{Mod}_{\mathcal{A}}^{g}

(A.32)

by doublets
(M_{g},\,r_{L})
with
M_{g}\in\mathcal{C}_{g}
and
r_{L}
a left map
r_{L}:\mathcal{A}_{0}\times M_{g}\to M_{g}
. The interesting part of the construction involves making these modules local. In particular the braiding map:

b:M_{g}\times\mathcal{A}_{0}\to\mathcal{A}_{0}\times M_{g}\,,

(A.33)

induces a nontrivial action of
g
onto the module morphism
r_{L}
sending

r_{L}(x_{i})\to\tilde{\eta}_{g}(x_{i})\,r_{L}(g(x_{i}))\,,

(A.34)

(A.36)

Or, in components:

r_{L}(x_{i})=\tilde{\eta}_{g}(x_{i})R_{x_{i},\,M_{g}}\cdot R_{M_{g},\,x_{i}}\cdot r_{L}(g(x_{i}))\,.

(A.37)

Thus the specification of
\tilde{\eta}
influences the structure of the
H
-twisted sectors after gauging
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

Appendix B
Lagrangian algebras for DW theories

In this appendix we prove that any Lagrangian algebra of
\text{DW}(\mathbb{A})
is of the form

\mathscr{L}_{\mathbb{B},[\nu]}=\left\{(b,\beta\psi_{\nu}(b)\ |\ b\in\mathbb{B}\ ,\ \beta\in N(\mathbb{B})\ \right\}

(B.1)

for some subgroup
\mathbb{B}\subset\mathbb{A}
and a class
[\nu]\in H^{2}(\mathbb{B},U(1))
, and that their associated boundary conditions correspond to a theory obtained from the electric boundary by gauging
\mathbb{B}
with discrete torsion
[\nu]
.

We denote by
\pi_{\mathbb{A}}:\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}
and
\pi_{\mathbb{A}^{\vee}}:\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{\vee}
the projections on the two factors. Let
\mathscr{L}\subset\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}
be Lagrangian. We define a subgroup of
\mathbb{A}

\mathbb{B}=\pi_{\mathbb{A}}(\mathscr{L})\subset\mathbb{A}\ .

(B.2)

Notice that this not necessarily a subgroup of
\mathscr{L}
. On the other hand any element of the form
(0,\beta)
with
\beta\in N(\mathbb{B})
has trivial braiding with any element of
\mathscr{L}
, hence since
\mathscr{L}
is maximal,
N(\mathbb{B})\subset\pi_{\mathbb{A}^{\vee}}(\mathscr{L})
must be a subgroup of
\mathscr{L}
. Using the short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow N(\mathbb{B})\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{\vee}\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}^{\vee}\longrightarrow 1

(B.3)

we realize any element of
\mathbb{A}^{\vee}
, and in particular of
\pi_{\mathbb{A}^{\vee}}(\mathscr{L})
, as a pair
\beta\omega
,
\beta\in N(\mathbb{B})
,
\omega\in\mathbb{B}^{\vee}
. All the elements of
\mathscr{L}
are then of the form
(b,\beta\omega)
,
b\in\mathbb{B}
,
\beta\in N(\mathbb{B})
,
\omega\in\mathbb{B}^{\vee}
, but since
|\mathscr{L}|=|\mathbb{A}|=|\mathbb{B}||N(\mathbb{B})|
there must exists a homomorphism
\psi:\mathbb{B}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}^{\vee}
such that

\omega=\psi(b)\ .

(B.4)

Finally
\psi
cannot be arbitrary in order
\mathscr{L}
to be Lagrangian, since the elements must have vanishing spin. Defining a bicharacter
\chi:\mathbb{B}\times\mathbb{B}\rightarrow U(1)
as
\chi(b_{1},b_{2})=\psi(b_{1})b_{2}
, then imposing that
(b,\beta\psi(b))
has trivial spin we get

1=\theta_{(b,\beta\psi(b))}=\chi(b,b)\ .

(B.5)

Thus
\chi
is alternating and defines a class
[\nu]\in H^{2}(\mathbb{B},\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
, hence
\mathscr{L}=\mathscr{L}_{\mathbb{B},[\nu]}
.

Now we aim to prove that the boundary defined by
\mathscr{L}_{\mathbb{B},[\nu]}
, where the symmetry is

\mathcal{S}=\frac{\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}}{\mathscr{L}_{\mathbb{B},[\nu]}}\cong\mathscr{L}_{\mathbb{B},[\nu]}^{\vee}

(B.6)

is obtained from the electric boundary by gauging
\mathbb{B}
with discrete torsion
[\nu]
. First we notice that
\mathscr{L}_{\mathbb{B},[\nu]}
is an extension of
\mathbb{B}
by
N(\mathbb{B})
determined as follows. Let
\widehat{c}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{B}^{\vee},N(\mathbb{B}))
be the class associated with

1\longrightarrow N(\mathbb{B})\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{\vee}\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}^{\vee}\longrightarrow 1\ .

(B.7)

This is determined by Pontryagin duality from
1\rightarrow\mathbb{B}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{B}\rightarrow 1
, which is associated with a class
c\in H^{2}(\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{B},\mathbb{B})
.
\widehat{c}
enters in the composition rules of elements of
\mathbb{A}^{\vee}
when they are represented as pairs
\beta\eta
,
\eta\in\mathbb{B}^{\vee},\beta\in N(\mathbb{B})
:

\beta_{1}\eta_{1}+\beta_{2}\eta_{2}=(\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\widehat{c}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2}))(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})\ .

(B.8)

The elements of
\mathscr{L}_{\mathbb{B},[\nu]}
can be realized as pairs of
b\in\mathbb{B}
and
\beta\in N(\mathbb{B})

[b,\beta]\equiv(b,\beta\psi_{\nu}(b))

(B.9)

and their composition is

\begin{array}[]{rl}[b_{1},\beta_{1}]+[b_{2},\beta_{2}]&=\Bigl[b_{1}+b_{2},\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}+\widehat{c}\left(\psi_{\nu}(b_{1}),\psi_{\nu}(b_{2})\right)\Bigr]\end{array}\ .

(B.10)

We conclude that
\mathscr{L}_{\mathbb{B},[\nu]}
is an extension

1\longrightarrow N(\mathbb{B})\longrightarrow\mathscr{L}_{\mathbb{B},[\nu]}\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}\longrightarrow 1

(B.11)

determined by

\psi_{\nu}^{*}(\,\widehat{c}\,)\in H^{2}(\mathbb{B},N(\mathbb{B}))\ .

(B.12)

Taking the Pontryagin dual of (
B.11
) we get

1\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}^{\vee}\overset{\iota}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{S}\overset{\pi}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{B}\longrightarrow 1

(B.13)

whose class is

\widetilde{c}=\psi_{\nu}\circ c\in H^{2}(\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{B},\mathbb{B}^{\vee})\ .

(B.14)

To show that this is the correct symmetry structure on the boundary theory obtained by gauging
\mathbb{B}
with discrete torsion
[\nu]
, we consider its partition function coupled to a background

B=\iota(B_{1})+s(B_{2})\in H^{1}(X,\mathcal{S})

(B.15)

where
s:\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
is a section of
\pi
and
B_{1},B_{2}
are gauge fields valued in
\mathbb{B}^{\vee}
and
\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{B}
respectively.
B_{2}
is closed but
B_{1}
it is not: its differential is equal to the pull-back through
B_{2}
of the extension class in
H^{2}(\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{B},\mathbb{B}^{\vee})
.
On the other hand the dynamical gauge field
B^{\prime}
valued in
\mathbb{B}
satisfies

dB^{\prime}=B_{2}^{*}(\,c\,)

(B.16)

and the partition function is

Z_{\mathbb{B},[\nu]}=\sum_{B^{\prime}}\exp{\left(\int_{X}\left(B^{\prime*}(\,\nu\,)+B_{1}\cup B^{\prime}\right)\right)}Z_{e}[B^{\prime},B_{2}]\ .

(B.17)

The exponent is not gauge invariant under
B^{\prime}\rightarrow d\rho
unless
B_{1}
satisfies

\psi_{\nu}(dB^{\prime})-dB_{1}=0\ .

(B.18)

This determines the modified cocycle condition for
B_{1}
as

dB_{1}=B_{2}^{*}(\,\widetilde{c}\,)

(B.19)

hence proving that
\mathcal{S}
is the right symmetry after gauging
\mathbb{B}
with discrete torsion
[\nu]
.

Appendix C
General duality-invariant Lagrangian algebras

In this appendix we report technical details regarding Lagrangian algebras
\mathscr{L}_{\mathbb{B},[\nu]}
for general
\mathbb{B}
. In particular we give the proof of their duality invariance and compute the mixed ’t Hooft anomaly with the invertible duality symmetry in that cases, extending the discussion for
\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{A}
given in the main text. For concreteness we look at the 2d/3d case, but the 4d/5d one is analogous.

C.1
Proof of duality invariance

In Section
3.3
we claimed the result for the general classification of duality invariant Lagrangian algebras of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{A})=\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{A}^{\vee}
. The most general Lagrangian algebra is in the form

\mathscr{L}_{\mathbb{B},[\nu]}=\left\{(b,\beta\psi_{\nu}(b))\ ,|\ b\in\mathbb{B}\ ,\beta\in N(\mathbb{B})\right\}

(C.1)

and this is duality invariant if and only if

1.

\phi\left(\text{Rad}(\nu)\right)=N(\mathbb{B})
.

2.

The injective homomorphism

\sigma:\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)\rightarrow\mathbb{A}/\text{Rad}(\nu)\ .

(C.2)

has image equal to
\sigma\left(\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)\right)=\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)
, and

\sigma^{2}=1\ .

(C.3)

To prove it we first notice that since
\mathscr{L}
and
\Phi(\mathscr{L})
are both Lagrangian, they are equal if and only if all their lines are mutually transparent. In other words for all
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}
,
\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{B})
we must have

\left[\phi(b^{\prime})b\right]\,\beta\left[\phi^{-1}(\beta^{\prime}\psi_{\nu}(b^{\prime}))\right]\,\psi_{\nu}(b)\left[\phi^{-1}\left(\beta^{\prime}\psi_{\nu}(b^{\prime})\right)\right]=1\ .

(C.4)

First we prove that the two conditions above are necessary. If (
C.4
) is satisfied, let us specialize it to
\beta=1
. If
b\in\text{Ker}(\psi_{\nu})
, then

\phi(b)b^{\prime}=1\,\ \ \ \forall b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{B}

(C.5)

implying that
\phi(b)\in\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{B})
. Thus
\phi(\text{Ker}(\psi_{\nu}))\subset\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{B})
. On the other hand, for generic
b
but
b^{\prime}=1
we have

\psi_{\nu}(b)\phi^{-1}(\beta^{\prime})=\chi_{\nu}(b,\phi^{-1}(\beta^{\prime}))=\left(\psi_{\nu}\left(\phi^{-1}(\beta^{\prime})\right)b\right)^{-1}=1

(C.6)

namely
\phi^{-1}(\beta^{\prime})\in\text{Ker}(\psi_{\nu})
. This proves that

\phi\left(\text{Ker}(\psi_{\nu})\right)=\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{B})

(C.7)

and thus we can define
\sigma:\mathbb{B}/\text{Ker}(\psi_{\nu})\rightarrow\mathbb{A}/\text{Ker}(\psi_{\nu})
as above. Let us now keep
\beta
generic but
\beta^{\prime}=1
. If also
b=1
we get

\beta\left(\sigma b^{\prime}\right)=1

(C.8)

which implies that
\sigma\left(\mathbb{B}/\text{Ker}(\psi_{\nu})\right)\subset\mathbb{B}/\text{Ker}(\psi_{\nu})
. On the other hand if
\beta=1
, for any
b,b^{\prime}
we have

\phi(b^{\prime})b=\psi_{\nu}\left(\sigma b^{\prime}\right)b

(C.9)

namely
\sigma^{2}=1
.

Conversely we can prove that these two conditions are also sufficient. Since
\phi^{-1}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{B}))\subset\mathbb{B}
and
\sigma\left(\mathbb{B}/\text{Ker}(\psi_{\nu})\right)\subset\mathbb{B}/\text{Ker}(\psi_{\nu})
, then
\beta\left(\phi^{-1}(\beta^{\prime})\right)=1
and
\beta(\sigma(b^{\prime}))=1
. Moreover since
\phi^{-1}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbb{B}))=\text{Ker}(\psi_{\nu})
we have

\psi_{\nu}(b)\left[\phi^{-1}(\beta^{\prime})\right]=\chi_{\nu}\left[b,\phi^{-1}(\beta^{\prime})\right]=\left[\psi_{\nu}\left(\phi^{-1}(\beta^{\prime})\right)b\right]^{-1}=1

(C.10)

and the equation (
C.4
) is reduced to

\left[\phi(b^{\prime})b\right]\left[\psi_{\nu}(b)\left(\sigma b^{\prime}\right)\right]=\gamma(b^{\prime},b)\chi_{\nu}(b,\sigma b^{\prime})=1

(C.11)

since the condition
\sigma^{2}=1
is equivalent to

\gamma(b^{\prime},b)=\chi_{\nu}(\sigma(b^{\prime}),b)

(C.12)

This completes the proof.

For what follows it is important to discuss few consequence of this theorem.

•

Because of
\phi(\text{Rad}(\nu))=N(\mathbb{B})
it follows that the short exact sequence
1\rightarrow\text{Rad}(\nu)\rightarrow\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}/\text{Rad}(\nu)\rightarrow 1
is the image under
\phi^{-1}
of
1\rightarrow N(\mathbb{B})\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}^{\vee}\rightarrow 1
. In other words there is a commutative diagram

(C.14)

•

It is simple to prove that there is a canonical isomorphism
N\left(\text{Rad}(\nu)\right)/N(\mathbb{B})\cong\left(\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)\right)^{\vee}
. Then using that
\phi(\mathbb{B})=N(\text{Rad}(\nu))
and
\phi(\text{Rad}(\nu))=N(\mathbb{B})
we found a commutative diagram

\displaystyle\hbox to477.85pt{\vbox to52.66pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 238.92352pt\lower-26.32838pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

(C.15)

as well as its Pontryagin dual

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{7}=S_{5}^{\vee}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)\right)^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Rad}(\nu)^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{8}=S_{6}^{\vee}:}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}/\text{Rad}(\nu)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\ .}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

(C.16)

C.2
Mixed anomaly in the general case

The discussion of this appendix is quite technical and involves heavy notation. We will use several short exact sequences which we denote as

S_{m}\ \ :\ \ \ \ 1\longrightarrow\mathbb{B}_{m}\overset{\iota_{m}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{A}_{m}\overset{\pi_{m}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{A}_{m}/\mathbb{B}_{m}\longrightarrow 1\ .

(C.17)

\iota_{m},\pi_{m},s_{m}
denote respectively the inclusion, the projection and a section of
\pi_{m}
.
S_{m}
induces an extension class
c_{m}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{A}_{m}/\mathbb{B}_{m},\mathbb{B}_{m})
. The sequences which will be used are the
S_{1},...,S_{8}
introduced in appendix
C.1
.

Moreover we will systematically decompose gauge fields valued in
\mathbb{A}_{m}
in terms of gauge fields values in the subgroup and the quotient as

a_{m}=\iota_{m}(b_{m})+s_{m}(b_{m}^{\prime})\ .

(C.18)

b_{m}^{\prime}
is closed while

db_{m}=b_{m}^{\prime*}(c_{m})\ .

(C.19)

All the fields have a subscript labelled the short exact sequence, and both the field valued in the subgroup and in the quotient are denoted by the same letter, but the one in the quotient is always primed. An important remark is in order. The relations (
C.19
) mean that in presence of a non-trivial extension the background for the subgroup is the sum of an ordinary cohomology class and a particular cochain solving the constraint, which is dependent on the background for the quotient. Hence all path integrals are intended to be done in order: one first integrate the background for the subgroup (the unprimed one), or more precisely its cohomology part, and then the background for the quotient (the primed one).

Let
\mathscr{L}_{D}
be a duality invariant algebra associated with
(\mathbb{B},[\nu])
. We want to compute the mixed anomaly between
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathbb{A})/\mathscr{L}_{D}
and the duality
G\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}
on the invertible boundary. This is obtained from the electric boundary by gauging
\mathbb{B}
with discrete torsion
[\nu]
. A gauge field
A\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{A})
can be decomposed according to the sequence
S_{3}
as

A=\iota_{3}(b_{3})+s_{3}(b_{3}^{\prime})

(C.20)

After gauging
\mathbb{B}
with torsion the dual symmetry
\mathcal{S}
is an extension of
\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{B}
by
\mathbb{B}^{\vee}
with extension class
\widetilde{c}=-\psi_{\nu}\circ c_{1}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{B},\mathbb{B}^{\vee})
(see appendix
B
), then a background field for
\mathcal{S}
is given by a pair
B,b^{\prime}_{3}
valued respectively in
\mathbb{B}^{\vee}
and
\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{B}
where

dB=b_{3}^{\prime*}(\widetilde{c})\ .

(C.21)

The partition function on the invertible boundary is

Z_{\text{inv}}[B,b^{\prime}_{3}]=\sum_{b_{3}}\exp{\left(2\pi i\int b_{3}^{*}(\nu)+2\pi i\int B\cup b_{3}\right)}Z_{e}[b_{3},b^{\prime}_{3}]\ .

(C.22)

By acting with the duality on the electric boundary we get the magnetic one, corresponding to the gauging of
\mathbb{A}
with trivial torsion:

\Phi\cdot Z_{e}[b_{3},b_{3}^{\prime}]=\sum_{a\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{A})}\exp{\left(2\pi i\int\phi(a)\cup\left(\iota_{3}(b_{3})+s_{3}(b^{\prime}_{3})\right)\right)}Z_{e}[a]\ .

(C.23)

We decompose the
\mathbb{A}
valued field
a
according to the sequence
S_{3}
:

a=\iota_{3}(a_{3})+s_{3}(a_{3}^{\prime})\ .

(C.24)

Because of the commutative diagram (
C.14
)
\phi(a)\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{A}^{\vee})
has a decomposition using
S_{4}
,

\phi(a)=\iota_{4}(x_{4})+s_{4}(x_{4}^{\prime})\ ,\ \ \ \ x_{4}=\phi(a_{3})\ ,\ \ x_{4}^{\prime}=\phi(a_{3}^{\prime})\ .

(C.25)

Furthermore it is useful to decompose
b_{3}
using
S_{5}

b_{3}=\iota_{5}(y_{5})+s_{5}(y_{5}^{\prime})\,.

(C.26)

Hence, using that
\nu
vanishes on
\text{Rad}(\nu)
, we have

\begin{array}[]{rl}\Phi\cdot Z_{\text{inv}}[B,b_{3}^{\prime}]=&\displaystyle\sum_{y_{5},y_{5}^{\prime},x_{4},x_{4}^{\prime}}\exp\biggl(2\pi i\int y_{5}^{\prime*}\nu+B\cup(\iota_{5}(y_{5})+s_{5}(y_{5}^{\prime}))+\\
&\displaystyle+\phi(a)\cup\Bigl(\iota_{3}\iota_{5}(y_{5})+\iota_{3}s_{5}(y_{5}^{\prime})+s_{3}(b_{3}^{\prime})\Bigr)\biggr)\;Z_{e}[a_{3},a_{3}^{\prime}]\end{array}

(C.27)

Since
y_{5}
appears linearly the sum over it gives a delta function imposing

\pi_{7}\left(B+\pi_{1}(\phi(a))\right)=0\ \ \longleftrightarrow\ \ B+\pi_{1}(\phi(a))\in\iota_{7}\left(\left(\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}\right)^{\vee}\right)

(C.28)

We notice that
\iota_{3}\iota_{5}=\iota_{2}
, and since
\phi(a)=\iota_{4}(x_{4})+s_{4}(x_{4}^{\prime})
(
C.28
) can be rewritten as

x_{4}^{\prime}=-\pi_{7}(B)\ .

(C.29)

This delta function will be solved by the
x_{4}^{\prime}
sum which however must be performed only after the
x_{4}
sum.

Now we can integrate out
y_{5}^{\prime}
. It appears quadratically hence the sum over it can be performed by solving its equation of motion

\psi_{\nu}(y_{5}^{\prime})+B+\pi_{1}(\phi(a))=0\ .

(C.30)

This equation can be inverted in virtue of (
C.28
) and using that
\psi_{\nu}:\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)\rightarrow\left(\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)\right)^{\vee}
is invertible. Plugging the result back we get

\Phi\cdot Z_{\text{inv}}[B,b_{3}^{\prime}]=\sum_{a_{3},a_{3}^{\prime}}\exp{\left(2\pi i\int\phi(a)\cup s_{3}(b_{3}^{\prime})-\Big(\psi_{\nu}^{-1}(B+\pi_{1}\phi(a))\Big)^{*}\nu\right)}\delta(x_{4}^{\prime}+\pi_{7}(B))Z_{e}[a]

(C.31)

We decompose
B
using
S_{7}
as

B=\iota_{7}(B_{7})+s_{7}(B_{7}^{\prime})

(C.32)

and the duality maps

\Phi(B_{7},B_{7}^{\prime},b_{3}^{\prime})=\left(\sigma^{\vee}(B_{7}),\phi(b_{3}^{\prime}),\phi^{-1}(B_{7}^{\prime})\right)\ .

(C.33)

The sum over
a_{3}^{\prime}
solves the delta function, while the one over
a_{3}
reconstructs
Z_{\text{inv}}
up to a multiplicative factor which gives the anomaly:

\Phi\cdot Z_{\text{inv}}[B_{7},B_{7}^{\prime},b_{3}^{\prime}]=\exp{\left(-\int\left(\phi^{-1}(\sigma^{\vee}B_{7})\right)^{*}\nu\right)}Z_{\text{inv}}\left(\sigma^{\vee}(B_{7}),\phi(b_{3}^{\prime}),\phi^{-1}(B_{7}^{\prime})\right)

(C.34)

Appendix D
Twisted cohomology and anomalies

Here we provide details on the topological actions in both 3d and 5d which we used to cancel the mixed anomaly between the duality symmetry and the 0-form (in 2d) or the 1-form (in 4d) symmetry, when we go to the invariant boundary. In the 2d case this is an anomaly for a semi-direct product, while in 4d is an anomaly for a split 2-group. In both cases we do not discuss the full anomaly, but only the one linear in the duality gauge field
A\in H^{d}(X,G)
which is relevant for our discussion.

D.1
Anomaly for a semi-direct product in 2d

We consider a semi-direct product
\mathbb{A}\rtimes_{\rho}G
, where
\rho:G\rightarrow\text{Aut}(\mathbb{A})
. This is associated with a short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}\overset{\iota}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{A}\rtimes_{\rho}G\overset{\pi}{\longrightarrow}G\longrightarrow 1

(D.1)

which splits, namely it admits a section
s:G\rightarrow\mathbb{A}\rtimes_{\rho}G
which is a group homomorphism. Any element can be written uniquey as
\iota(a)s(g)
,
a\in\mathbb{A}
,
g\in G
, with product rule

\iota(a_{1})s(g_{1})\times\iota(a_{2})s(g_{2})=\iota(a_{1}+\rho_{g_{1}}(a_{2}))s(g_{1}+g_{2})\ .

(D.2)

In particular

s(g)\iota(a)s(g^{-1})=\iota(\rho_{g}(a))\ .

(D.3)

Semi-direct products are generically non-abelian, hence we only have 1-form gauge fields. These are classes
\mathcal{A}\in H^{1}(X,\mathbb{A}\rtimes_{\rho}G)
, namely

(d\mathcal{A})_{ijk}=\mathcal{A}_{jk}\mathcal{A}_{ik}^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{ij}=1\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \mathcal{A}_{ij}\sim\Lambda_{i}^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{ij}\Lambda_{j}

(D.4)

where the order of multiplication matters. Since
\mathcal{A}_{ij}\in\mathbb{A}\rtimes_{\rho}G
, we can write

\mathcal{A}_{ij}=\iota(B_{ij})s(A_{ij})

(D.5)

where
B\in C^{1}(X,\mathbb{A})
,
A\in C^{1}(X,G)
. Using the commutation relation (
D.3
), the cocycle condition
(d\mathcal{A})_{ijk}=1
is equivalent to

(dA)_{ijk}=A_{jk}-A_{ik}+A_{ij}=0\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ (d_{\rho(A)}B)_{ijk}=\rho_{A_{ij}}B_{jk}-B_{ik}+B_{ij}=0\ .

(D.6)

The identification
\mathcal{A}_{ij}\sim\Lambda_{i}^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{ij}\Lambda_{j}
, upon decomposing
\Lambda_{i}=\iota(\theta_{i})s(\lambda_{i})
become

A_{ij}\sim A_{ij}-\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{j}\ ,\ \ \ \ B_{ij}\sim\rho_{\lambda_{i}}^{-1}\left(B_{ij}+\rho_{A_{ij}}\theta_{j}-\theta_{i}\right)\ .

(D.7)

Hence
A
defines a class in cohomology group
H^{1}(X,G)
, while
B
a class in the
twisted
cohomology group
H^{1}_{\rho}(X,\mathbb{A})
, also called cohomology with local coefficients.

We are interested in the anomaly for
\mathbb{A}\rtimes_{\rho}G
whose 3d inflow action is quadratic in
B
and ”linear” in
A
. The word linear is in quote since
B
is a twisted class, and thus
A
will appear not only linearly, but also in the twisting. This anomaly is characterized by a characteristic class of
\mathbb{A}\rtimes_{\rho}G
bundles taking value in

H^{1}_{\rho}(G,H^{2}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}))

[
139
]
. Such a class can be thought of as a function
\mu
on
G
with values in the group of alternating bicharacter over
\mathbb{A}
, satisfying

\rho_{g}\mu(h)+\mu(g)=\mu(g+h)\ .

(D.8)

The
G
-action on bicharacters is given by (
3.57
).
\mu
is subject to the identification

\mu(g)\sim\mu(g)+\rho_{g}\xi-\xi\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \xi\in H^{2}(\mathbb{A}^{\vee},\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})\ .

(D.9)

Notice that
\mu(0)=0
, so that
\mu(-g)=-\rho_{g}^{-1}\mu(g)
.

Given
A\in H^{1}(X,G)
we construct
\mu(A)\in C^{1}(X,H^{2}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}))
, which satisfies the twisted cocycle condition

\left(d_{\rho(A)}\mu(A)\right)_{ijk}=\rho_{A_{ij}}\mu(A_{jk})-\mu(A_{ik})+\mu(A_{ij})=\mu(A_{ij}+A_{jl})-\mu(A_{ik})=0\ .

(D.10)

Moreover under a gauge transformation
A\rightarrow A+d\lambda
is changes by

\begin{array}[]{rl}\mu(A_{ij})\rightarrow\mu(A_{ij}+\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{i})=&\rho_{\lambda_{i}}^{-1}\mu(A_{ij}+\lambda_{j})+\mu(-\lambda_{i})=\\
=&\rho_{\lambda_{i}}^{-1}\left(\rho_{A_{ij}}(\mu(\lambda_{j}))-\mu(\lambda_{i})+\mu(A_{ij})\right)=\\
=&\rho_{\lambda_{i}}^{-1}\left(\left(d_{\rho(A)}\mu(\lambda)\right)_{ij}+\mu(A_{ij})\right)\end{array}\ ,

(D.11)

hence
\mu(A)\in H^{1}_{\rho}(X,H^{2}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}))
.

Given
B\in H^{1}_{\rho}(X,\mathbb{A}^{\vee})
we can form the cup product
\mu(A)\cup B\cup B\in H^{3}(X,\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
as

\left(\mu(A)\cup B\cup B\right)_{ijkl}=\mu(A_{ij})\left(\rho_{A_{ij}}B_{jk},\rho_{A_{ik}}B_{kl}\right)\ .

(D.12)

Under a gauge variation
A\rightarrow A+d\lambda
we have

\begin{array}[]{rl}\left(\mu(A)\cup B\cup B\right)_{ijkl}\rightarrow&\rho_{\lambda_{i}}^{-1}\left(\mu(A_{ij})+\left(d_{\rho(A)}\mu(\lambda)\right)_{ij}\right)\Big(\rho_{\lambda_{i}}^{-1}\rho_{A_{ij}}B_{jk},\rho_{\lambda_{i}}^{-1}\rho_{A_{ik}}B_{kl}\Big)\\
\\
=&\left(\mu(A)\cup B\cup B\right)_{ijkl}+\left(d_{\rho(A)}\mu(\lambda)\right)_{ij}\Big(\rho_{A_{ij}}B_{jk},\rho_{A_{ik}}B_{kl}\Big)\ .\end{array}

(D.13)

This means that

\mu(A)\cup B\cup B\rightarrow\mu(A)\cup B\cup B+d_{\rho(A)}(\mu(\lambda))\cup B\cup B=\mu(A)\cup B\cup B+d\left(\mu(\lambda)\cup B\cup B\right)\ .

(D.14)

We write the inflow action as

S_{\mu}=2\pi i\int_{X_{3}}\mu(A)\cup B\cup B\ .

(D.15)

If
X_{3}
this is gauge invariant, while if
\partial X_{3}=X_{2}
we get a boundary term

S_{\mu}\rightarrow S_{\mu}+2\pi i\int_{X_{2}}\mu(\lambda)\cup B\cup B\ .

(D.16)

D.2
Anomaly for a split 2-group in 4d

In 4d we have an analog story, where
\mathbb{A}
is now a 1-form symmetry. The full symmetry structure is a split 2-group, which is a higher categorical version of a semi-direct product. The definitions can be found in
[
121
]
and a more physical discussion is in
[
120
,
108
]
. Here we simply use two facts which from our viewpoint can be motivated as being the straightforward generalization of the discussion on semi-direct products.

First, a background field for the split 2-group is an ordinary cohomology class
A\in H^{1}(X,G)
and a twisted cohomology class
B\in H^{2}_{\rho}(X,\mathbb{A})
. The latter means that

\left(d_{\rho(A)}B\right)_{ijkl}=\rho_{A_{ij}}B_{jkl}-B_{ikl}+B_{ijl}-B_{ijk}=0

(D.17)

and there is an identification (gauge transformations)

A_{ij}\sim A_{ij}+\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{i}\ ,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ B_{ijk}\sim\rho_{\lambda_{i}}^{-1}\left(B_{ijk}+\rho_{A_{ij}}\theta_{jk}-\theta_{ik}+\theta_{ij}\right)

(D.18)

which are the obvious generalizations of (
D.7
).

Second, the piece of the anomaly for the split 2-group which is ”linear” in
A
and quadratic in
B
is labelled by a characteristic class of 2-group gauge bundles

\mu\in H^{1}_{\rho}(G,H^{4}(B^{2}\mathbb{A},\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}))\ .

(D.19)

One can show
[
120
]
that
H^{4}(B^{2}\mathbb{A},\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
is isomorphic to
\Gamma(\mathbb{A})^{\vee}
, the Pontryagin dual of the universal quadratic group of
\mathbb{A}
, which can be identified with the group of quadratic functions
q:\mathbb{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
(see
[
120
,
108
]
for precise definitions and details). The
G
action on them is naturally given by

(\rho_{g}q)(a)=q\left(\rho_{g}^{-1}a\right)\ .

(D.20)

The construction of the 5d anomaly inflow is very similar to the semi-direct product, thus we skip many details. Given
A\in H^{1}(X,G)
we construct
\mu(A)
which satisfies (
D.10
) and (
D.11
), thus defines a class in
H^{1}_{\rho}(X,\Gamma(\mathbb{A})^{\vee})
. Recall that
H^{4}(B^{2}\mathbb{A},\Gamma(\mathbb{A}))\cong\text{Hom}(\Gamma(\mathbb{A}),\Gamma(\mathbb{A}))
has a distinguished element
\mathfrak{P}
, the Pontryagin square, such that

B\in H_{\rho}^{2}(X,\mathbb{A})\ ,\ \ \ \ B^{*}\mathfrak{P}=\mathfrak{P}_{\rho}(B)\in H_{\rho}^{4}(X,\Gamma(\mathbb{A}))\ .

(D.21)

The action of
G
on
\Gamma(\mathbb{A})
is induced by the one on
\Gamma(\mathbb{A})^{\vee}
in such a way to make the natural pairing
\langle\ ,\ \rangle:\Gamma(\mathbb{A})\times\Gamma(\mathbb{A})^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
invariant. Under
A\rightarrow A+d\lambda
the latter transforms as

\mathfrak{P}_{\rho}(B)_{i_{0},...,i_{4}}\rightarrow\rho_{\lambda_{i_{0}}}^{-1}\mathfrak{P}_{\rho}(B)_{i_{0},...,i_{4}}\ .

(D.22)

Using the pairing between
\Gamma(\mathbb{A})
and
\Gamma(\mathbb{A})^{\vee}
we construct
\mu(A)\cup\mathfrak{P}_{\rho}(B)\in H^{5}(X,\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z})
as

\left(\mu(A)\cup\mathfrak{P}_{\rho}(B)\right)_{i_{0},...,i_{5}}=\Big\langle\mu(A)_{i_{0}i_{1}},\rho_{A_{i_{0}i_{1}}}\mathfrak{P}_{\rho}(B)_{i_{0},...,i_{4}}\Big\rangle

(D.23)

Under
A\rightarrow A+d\lambda
we have

\mu(A)\cup\mathfrak{P}_{\rho}(B)\rightarrow\mu(A)\cup\mathfrak{P}_{\rho}(B)+d\Big(\mu(\lambda)\cup\mathfrak{P}_{\rho}(B)\Big)\ .

(D.24)

We conclude that the 5d inflow action is

S_{\mu}=2\pi i\int_{X_{5}}\mu(A)\cup\mathfrak{P}_{\rho}(B)\ .

(D.25)

Appendix E
Equivariantization for 2-algebras

In this Appendix we show how the symmetry fractionalization datum
\eta
appears in theory of equivariantization for symmetric 2-algebras
[
140
,
70
]
.
We refer to there works for a complete introduction to the formalism and we limit ourselves to highlight the important steps for our application. We will assume that all objects are invertible for simplicity. We stress that a full definition of the equivariantization procedure is still an open problem which should be object of future study.

In five dimensions 2-categories are sylleptic
[
141
]
, meaning that there exists a 2-morphism
\sigma
such the braiding 1-morphism
b:X\times Y\to Y\times X
satisfies:

(E.1)

Physically
\sigma(X,Y)
encodes the braiding data between five dimensional surfaces
B_{X,Y}=\sigma(X,Y)/\sigma(Y,X)
.
A symmetric two-algebra
\mathcal{A}^{[2]}
describes the gauging of a 2-categorical symmetry in five dimensions, is it described by:
57
57

57

We omit the unit morphisms and its various higher morphisms for the sake of simplicity, since they will not play a role in our presentation.

•

An object
\mathcal{A}=\displaystyle\bigoplus_{x_{i}\,\text{simple}}Z_{x}\,x\,\in\,\mathcal{C}
. We will assume
Z_{x}=0,\,1
.

•

A one-morphisms
m:\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
.

•

Two two-isomorphisms
\mu,\beta
which uplift the associativity and commutativity relations for
m
to

\displaystyle\hbox to237.56pt{\vbox to50.71pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 118.77774pt\lower-24.51248pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-118.77774pt}{-19.49306pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

(E.2)

\mu
and
\beta
are furthermore subject to various higher algebraic identities which we do not report.
A zero-form symmetry
G
acts on the algebra
\mathcal{A}
, more specifically:

•

To an object
x\in\mathcal{A}
we associate a one-isomorphism (an invertible line)
\varphi_{g}(x):x\to g(x)
localized on the
U_{g}
surface.

•

To the algebra morphism
m
we associate a two-morphism
\mu_{g}(x,y):\varphi_{g}(x)\times\varphi_{g}(y)\to\varphi_{g}(x\times y)
. Similarly for
\beta
.

•

To an algebra 2-isomorphism we associate an identity for the equivariantization data.

All these data must be compatible with the natural multiplicative structure for
G
defects. In particular we can consider the action of
ghk\simeq(gh)k\simeq g(hk)\in G
on the algebra
\mathcal{A}
. Each three-valent junction
g\times h\to gh
defines a 2-isomorphism
\eta_{g,h}(x):\varphi_{g}(x)\times\varphi_{h}(g(x))\to\varphi_{gh}(x)
. Clearly
\eta
is best though of as a two-cochain
\eta(g,h)
with values in the Pontryagin dual of
\mathcal{A}
,
C^{2}(G,\,\mathcal{A}^{\vee})
.
58
58

58

As in the case of three-dimensions, the correct statement is that it is a torsor over such group.
Consistency of
\eta
with the associativity of the
G
defects implies (we draw below an horizontal section of the configuration)

\displaystyle\rho_{g}\eta(h,k)\,\eta(g,hk)=\eta(g,h)\,\eta(gh,k)\,,\ \ \ \rho_{g}\eta(h,k)[x]=\eta_{h,k}(g(x))\,

\eta
defines a closed two-cochain with values in the dual of
\mathcal{A}
.
59
59

59

As in the 3d case, the corrects statement is that it is a torsor over such group.
This datum matches the symmetry fractionalization data we have described in Section
3.4
.

Appendix F
First obstruction and invariant TQFTs

In this last appendix we review the relationship between the first obstruction and the existence of duality-invariant TQFTs with symmetry
\mathbb{A}
. This was explained in
[
13
]
for the case of the Tambara-Yamagami category
\text{TY}(\mathbb{A})_{\gamma,\epsilon}
and in
[
49
]
for 4d theories with
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
1-form symmetry.

Self-dual TQFTs in 2d.

A two-dimensional TQFT with symmetry
\mathbb{A}
can be characterized by its unbroken subgroup
\mathbb{B}
(in the sense of spontaneous symmetry breaking) and an SPT phase for
\mathbb{B}
described by
\nu\in H^{2}\bigl(B\mathbb{B},U(1)\bigr)
. The partition function is as in (
3.19
):

Z[B]=\begin{cases}\exp\bigl(2\pi i\int\!B^{*}\nu\bigr)\qquad&\text{if }\pi(B)=0\;,\\
0&\text{otherwise}\;,\end{cases}

(F.1)

where
\pi
is the projection in the short exact sequence
1\to\mathbb{B}\xrightarrow{i\,}\mathbb{A}\xrightarrow{\pi\,}\mathbb{A}/\mathbb{B}\to 1
. The duality action on the TQFT is defined as

\mathcal{N}\cdot Z[B]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\rvert H^{1}(X,\mathbb{A})\rvert}}\sum_{a\,\in\,H^{1}(X,\mathbb{A})}\exp\biggl(2\pi i\int_{X}a\cup_{\gamma}B\biggr)\,Z[a]\;.

(F.2)

Let us assume that
\nu
satisfies (
3.13
)–(
3.15
). We evaluate the above equation on the torus
T^{2}
, however this can equivalently be done on any Riemann surface by choosing a decomposition of
H_{1}(\Sigma_{g})
in A- and B-cycles. We find:

\mathcal{N}\cdot Z[B_{1},\,B_{2}]=\frac{1}{\lvert\mathbb{A}\rvert}\sum_{a_{1},a_{2}\,\in\,\mathbb{B}}\chi_{\nu}(a_{1},a_{2})\,\gamma(a_{1},B_{2})\,\gamma(a_{2},B_{1})^{-1}\;,

(F.3)

where we used that
Z[a]=0
if
\pi(a)\neq 0
to restrict the sum. We first perform the sum over the subgroup
\text{Rad}(\nu)
of
\mathbb{B}
. This is zero unless:

B\in\phi^{-1}\left(N(\text{Rad}(\nu))\right)\,,

(F.4)

this group coincides with
\mathbb{B}
(and thus would give to correct delta function) if and only if
\phi(\text{Rad}(\nu))=N(\mathbb{B})
.
60
60

60

One uses the fact that, for any subgroup
\mathbb{B}
,
\phi^{-1}N(\phi^{-1}N(\mathbb{B}))=\mathbb{B}
.
Thus (
3.13
) ensures that preserved subgroup is the same after the action of
\mathcal{N}
.
Finally we perform the sum over (say)
a_{1}
restricted to
\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)
which fixes
a_{2}=\sigma B_{2}
thus:

\mathcal{N}\cdot Z[B_{1},B_{2}]=\gamma\bigl(\sigma B_{2},B_{1})^{-1}\,\delta\bigl(\pi(B_{1})\bigr)\,\delta\bigl(\pi(B_{2})\bigr)=Z[B_{1},B_{2}]\;,

(F.5)

where (
3.14
) and (
3.15
) ensure that the SPT phase for
\mathbb{B}
remains the same.

Self-dual TQFTs in 4d.

A similar reasoning is valid in 4d. Without assuming whether the four manifold
X
is spin we can defined a 4d
\mathbb{A}^{(1)}
TQFT by specifying the preserved subgroup
\mathbb{B}
and an
\mathbb{B}
SPT:

\nu\in H^{2}(B^{2}\mathbb{B},\,U(1))\,,

(F.6)

and define

Z[B]=\begin{cases}\exp\left(2\pi i\int B^{*}\nu\right)\,,\ \ \ &\text{if}\ \pi(B)=0\\
0\,,\ \ \ &\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

(F.7)

We then have
61
61

61

This normalization makes sense on manifolds without torsion 2-cycles, on which we can trade it for the common one
[
73
]
by an Euler number counterterm.

\mathcal{N}\cdot Z[B]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{H^{2}(X,\mathbb{B})}}\sum_{a\,\in\,H^{1}(X,\,\mathbb{A})}\exp\left(2\pi i\int_{X}a\cup_{\gamma}B\right)\,\exp\left(2\pi i\int q_{\nu}(a)\right)\,.

(F.8)

First we perform the sum over
\text{Rad}(\nu)
. Since the bicharacter vanishes identically when evaluated on such elements it can be argued that its refinement
q
also does, independently on the choice of characteristic element. The sum then becomes linear and sets the result to zero unless

B\in\phi^{-1}N(\text{Rad}(\nu))=\mathbb{B}\,.

(F.9)

The sum over the quotient
\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)
is quadratic and we solve it by shifting
a\to a+\sigma(B)
, which decouples the two fields owning to

\chi_{\nu}(\sigma(a),\,b)=\gamma(a,\,b)^{-1}\,.

(F.10)

We are left with:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\cdot Z[B]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}G_{\nu}\,\exp\left(2\pi i\int B^{*}\left[2\nu+\nu\circ\sigma\right]\right)\,,\ \ \ &\text{if}\,\pi(B)=0\,,\\
0\,,\ \ \ &\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\,,

(F.11)

\displaystyle G_{\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{H^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu))}}\sum_{a\,\in\,H^{2}(X,\,\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu))}\exp\left(2\pi i\int a^{*}\nu\right)\,.

If
X
is spin the quadratic refinement does not depend on the choice of characteristic element and we can lift identities for
\chi_{\nu}
to identities for
q
. Since

\chi_{\nu}(\sigma a,\,\sigma b)=\chi_{\nu}(a,b)^{-1}\,,

(F.12)

on spin manifolds
\nu\circ\sigma=-\nu
as classes. Furthermore, it is possible
[
49
]
to prove that the Gauss sum
G_{\nu}
is unity. We find that the TQFT
Z[B]
is duality-invariant on spin manifolds. On the other hand, on non-spin manifolds, we need to impose the stronger condition:

q_{\nu}(\sigma B)=q_{\nu}(B)^{-1}\,,\ \ \ B\in\mathbb{B}/\text{Rad}(\nu)\,

(F.13)

on the quadratic refinement of
\chi_{\nu}
. A similar story applies to triality defects with minimal modifications.
